# Canary — islam.museum release notes

The museum's public release notes: every change that lands on `main`, recorded in
reader's language in the same commit that makes the change. Newest release first.
The site build reads the version and date from the top block below and shows them
in the footer of every page and on the changelog page (`/{lang}/changelog/`).

Rules for every agent and contributor (`docs/engineering/15-release-notes-and-versioning.md`):
append your entry under the current version block, grouped by kind — Added, Changed,
Fixed, Removed, Performance, Tests, Data, Breaking, Rolled back. Write what changed
and why it matters, never a bare commit message. If your change bumps the version,
open a new block on top with the new version and today's date.

---

## v1.3.0 — 2026-11-06

### Added
- Nine modern figures from the modern-figures lane's first Persian-language harvest join the People wing (wave 194): the Iranian lecturers Alireza Panahian (1.35 million Instagram followers) and Ali Akbar Raefipour (1.00 million), the Qur'an commentator Hossein Ansarian (853,000), the eulogists Mahdi Rasuli (1.55 million), Seyed Majid Banifatemeh (1.18 million), Mohammad Hossein Pouyanfar (615,000), Abdolreza Helali (508,000) and Mahmoud Karimi (439,000), and the Ethiopian Amharic preacher Abubaker Ahmed (240,300 on TikTok) — each with sourced pages in Arabic, English and Urdu, every audience figure a dated platform read from build day. Six further names from the same harvest were held to the watch list because their live counts could not be verified at the lane's bar on the day.
- **Inventions Browser97 — Karkh market relocation.** Adds an Arabic-first, English and Urdu account of the market move attributed to al-Mansur in773CE, distinguishing the stated security rationale from any unproved outcome; reuses the historical city map only as context and preserves the arcades' documented later guard use.
- **Quran.com discovery plan (`docs/plan/28-quran-com-enrichment.md`).** Records the founder's choice, on 6 November 2026, to use Quran.com's public catalogs only to discover which commentary, translation and reciter editions exist, checked against books. The Developer Terms bar raw API data in `data/` and caching beyond seven days, so no API text enters the museum's data; the plan lists the phases and what stays out.
- **Tafsir Browser search (Phase 4).** The Tafsir Browser's volume pages are now indexed the same way as the landing page: the volume header is searchable by name, and the long verse list on each volume page is kept out of the search index so it is not flooded with verse numbers. A test guards the markup.
- Inventions Browser94: Baghdad's commissioned round-city nucleus plan in Arabic, English and Urdu, crediting older circular-city precedents and distinguishing foundation, occupation and completion. Source disagreements remain explicit; the inspected1900map is a reconstruction, not a762survey. Existing maker/city accounts and political perspectives preserved.
- Tafsir Browser (phase 3): `/api/v1/tafsir.json` exports the Jami' al-Bayan commentary and its volumes 1 to 9 with their verse coverage, documented in the data guide, the OpenAPI file and llms.txt. Submission-impacting: the data-extraction route, with the founder's sign-off in chat.
- Inventions Browser93: al-Mahdi's central zimam oversight, in Arabic, English and Urdu, distinguishing departmental account checks from later central coordination. Earlier seal practices, individual identity evidence and political accounts remain separate; the inspected nineteenth-century ledger cover is context, not an Abbasid form or a claim of modern independent audit.
- Inventions Browser91: Muawiya's seal-and-copy registry, Arabic/English/Urdu account of checking, sealing and reference copies, with older administrative influences and attribution limits. Modern sealed letters are explicit context, not an original artifact or proof of historic wax; all earlier maker perspectives and registry prose remain intact.
- Inventions Browser92: Abd al-Malik's Arabic fiscal-register reform, full Arabic/English/Urdu account of phased language transfer, translators and inherited expertise; earlier bilingual papyrus is context, not a reform register. Existing political assessments and all prior invention content preserved.
- Tafsir Browser (phase 0): the proposal, the plan document `docs/plan/27-tafsir-browser.md`, and the register, plan-index, roadmap, lane and status entries for a separate wing of Qur'an commentaries, Tabari first. Approved in chat by the founder on 6 November 2026; nothing is live until the founder deploys.
- Inventions Browser90: Abd al-Malik's epigraphic gold-coin standard in Arabic, English and Urdu, with older and transitional coinages credited, source-date differences visible, and an inspected public-domain later dinar distinguished from the first issue. No invention-of-money or modern-value claim.
- Inventions Browser89: Umar's recurring military stipend register, Arabic-first/English/Urdu account of annual grants, monthly provisions, older administrative precedents and competing transmitted allocations; inspected Abu Yusuf pages/variant note, with contextual receipt explicitly not an original stipend ledger.
- Inventions Browser88: Hijri-era administrative dating under Umar, full Arabic/English/Urdu account of consultation, adoption versus epoch, source-date variants and later arithmetic limits; inspected public-domain PERF558 receipt context, not a founding decree. Existing maker identity, perspectives and prose retained.
- Ibn Sina's seven conditions for assessing drug effects now have an Arabic, English and Urdu account, with the original Arabic pages compared against a scholarly translation; humoral theory, translation limits and disputed affiliation stay visible rather than being recast as a modern clinical trial.
- Invention86: Ibn Zuhr's reported animal-recovery investigation, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with al-Taysir and preserved biography. It credits the older surgical tradition and distinguishes the animal report from a human trial or safety guarantee, without operative instructions. Source disagreements remain attributed; the modern CC0goat is context, not the experimental animal.
- Invention85: al-Biruni's proposed solar-gearing simplification, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its astrolabe treatise and additive biography links. The older calendar's unknown maker stays credited; manual input, approximate lunar cycles, inferred assembly and the360/364day discrepancy remain explicit. Checked ratios do not turn simplification into guaranteed accuracy, and the public-domain modern tooth detail is not a historical reconstruction.
- Invention84: al-Farisi's factorization-based approach to proving an older amicable-number rule, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its treatise and additive biography links. Earlier credit and disputed proof/priority remain explicit. Checked modern examples explain both directions and prime conditions; the licensed modern rod diagram is not a medieval artifact.
- Invention83: Abu Kamil's systematic search for integer answers, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with the Taraif and preserved biography. Earlier traditions, disputed transmission, titles and manuscript-count differences stay visible. Checked modern examples distinguish positive from nonnegative answers without claiming to settle historical number variants; the licensed modern pigeon is context, not market evidence.
- Invention82: Ibn al-Haytham's factorial construction for a simultaneous-remainder problem, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with preserved biographical links.721 is one answer, not the least; the independently checked modern family is not presented as his unreproduced second method. Older remainder theory and later theorem naming remain distinct; a licensed modern clock image is analogy, not historical apparatus.
- Invention81: al-Mahani's successive-quotient account of proportional magnitudes, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its ratio treatise and preserved biography. The inherited Euclidean process, unresolved textual witnesses and missing equivalence proof stay visible. Checked modern examples distinguish complete sequences from shared prefixes; the licensed modern tiling is not his manuscript or notation.
- Invention80: al-Mahani's algebraic formulation of Archimedes' sphere-division problem, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its commentary and a richer preserved biography. The formulation is not falsely presented as his completed solution. Checked modern cap-volume examples distinguish volume ratios, complementary pieces and physically possible roots; the public-domain modern diagram is not a manuscript reconstruction.
- Invention79: al-Farisi's water-sphere explanation of the two rainbow paths, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with his biography and the Tanqih. The glass approximation, failed colour theory, teacher-priority debate and disputed completion date stay visible. A licensed modern Alaska photograph is context, not a surviving apparatus; modern path symbols do not pretend to reproduce manuscript notation.
- Invention76: Abu al-Wafa's complement-based multiplication with a debt intermediate, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu alongside his practical arithmetic book. The example distinguishes a negative step from a positive final answer without claiming the first negative numbers; differing book dates remain visible. A licensed modern number line is comparison, not his notation.
- Invention77: al-Kashi's general numerical root extraction, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with additive links to his biography and Key to Arithmetic. Earlier al-Karaji-school and al-Samawal methods remain visible; comparison with Horner is not a claim of first roots or proven transmission. Independently checked modern cube-root bounds and a licensed modern cube clarify the topic without pretending to reconstruct an unread historical table.
- Invention78: Ibn Muadh's twilight-based estimate of atmospheric height, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its treatise and a richer sourced biography. The corrected authorship, uncertain birth and death after1079remain explicit;historical52miles is not a modern atmosphere ceiling. A simplified checked geometric example and licensed modern twilight diagram are clearly separated from the lost original and its reported translations.
- Invention75: Ibn al-Haytham's integrated incoming-light theory of vision, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with additive links to his biography and Book of Optics. The account separates sensation from interpretation and incoming-light insight from mistaken crystalline-humour physiology and idealized eye geometry. A licensed modern eye diagram is explicitly comparison, not his anatomy; earlier camera/reflection contributions remain separate.
- Invention74: al-Kashi's modular calculation of muqarnas surface areas, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with the Key to Arithmetic and preserved maker biography. The record distinguishes measuring an older craft from inventing it, squared unit conversion from length conversion, and curved-surface averages from exact measurement. The historian's projection criticism and differing recomputations remain visible; the licensed modern Qom photograph is not attributed to al-Kashi or evidence of using his method.
- Invention73: Khayyam's quadrilateral analysis of parallels, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its commentary and additive maker enrichment. Equal perpendicular legs and three summit-angle cases remain conditional on admitted premises, not an independent proof of Euclid's fifth postulate or priority for complete modern non-Euclidean systems. The modern public-domain diagram's different lettering and schematic limits are explicit; the independently checked coordinate example assumes a Euclidean plane.
- Invention72: Ibn al-Shatir's complete quadrant,full Arabic/English/Urdu invention/work with additive maker enrichment and unchanged existing object linked. Distinct from his lunar model,sundial,Alai quadrant and universal astrolabe;universal usefulness is an attributed aim,not measured perfect accuracy. Fathi's actual public2015introduction read,not full edition/manuscripts. Context photo's CC BY-SA3.0verified,modern restored cord/plummet and non-original identity explicit;independent paper examples only.
- Invention71: Sharaf al-Din al-Tusi's positive-root analysis for cubic equations, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its work and preserved maker biography. The maximum-condition method is separate from Khayyam's constructions and the linear astrolabe; independently checked modern examples distinguish no positive root from no real root, and repeated from distinct roots. Derivative interpretations remain attributed, not a claim to invent all modern calculus; the licensed modern graph has a different stated polynomial.
- Invention70: Sharaf al-Din al-Tusi's linear astrolabe, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its maker enriched. The staff is distinguished from Nasir al-Din's work and Jacob's staff; a later translated witness preserves its azimuth limits, and the modern geometry image is not a reconstruction. The maker card's unsupported Maragha employment conflation is corrected without losing the original import attribution.
- Invention69: Ammar al-Mawsili's described hollow cataract needle, with full Arabic/English/Urdu text and a sourced maker biography. The author's claims, manuscript variants and later historians' doubts stay distinct; a modern licensed anatomy image supplies context, not an original needle, and the historical account gives no surgical instructions.
- Invention68: al-Khujandi's Fakhri sextant at Rayy, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with its maker linked. The original printed account, differing numerical witnesses and Biruni's stability criticism stay visible: a finer scale is not guaranteed accuracy, and the modern geometry image is not a reconstruction or a nautical sextant plan.
- Invention67: Taqi al-Din's seconds-reading astronomical mechanical clock, fully in Arabic/English/Urdu with preserved maker links. Earlier European clocks remain visible, seconds display is distinguished from measured accuracy, and the modern icon is not presented as a surviving Ottoman clock.
- Invention66: al-Kashi's plate of conjunctions, with full Arabic/English/Urdu explanation and preserved maker biography. The linear-interpolation instrument is distinct from his plate of heavens, sine and circumference work; hypothetical modern numbers explain the task without claiming original graduations, global priority or software ancestry.
- The Modern Islam Browser gets its data model (plan 26, Phase 1, approved by the founder on 6 November 2026 as D-000): a twenty-fourth class, `award`, for prizes, medals, sales certifications, rankings, titles and competition prizes; four optional sourced blocks on every record, `honours` (who gave what to whom and when, with the result), `footprint` (the Wikipedia languages, Wikidata item, catalogue ids and dated press coverage of a subject), `reach` (platform figures read on a date, never stated as facts) and `horizon` (what is announced, in production, under construction or scheduled, with its expected date); `sports` on a person, `tracks` on an album, `scope` and `countries` on a group; new kinds (album, song, protest, attack, competition, award ceremony, festival, heritage month, exhibition, tournament, launch, newspaper, television channel, radio station, newsletter, channel, record label, production company, device, product, chain, grocery, airline, hotel, manufacturer, conglomerate, team, band, think tank, online community, Islamic centre, museum, university campus) and roles (singer, composer, actor, director, producer, presenter, journalist, diplomat, banker, economist, entrepreneur, comedian, influencer, coach, astronaut, judge, lawyer, nurse, professor); nine predicates (performed, composed, produced, played for, represented, owns, organised, hosted, and the derived `awarded`). A record's browser section is derived from its class, kind and dates, never typed; `npm run modern:census` counts the existing records per section into `docs/modern-islam/coverage.md`. The static API gains `/api/v1/award/index.json`, `/api/v1/modern/index.json`, one file per section, one per year from 1976, and the reach indices `/api/v1/modern/top/<list>.json` with their printed formula. No award record, no page and no content yet; nothing is live until the founder deploys, and no Worker or workflow file changed.
- Validator rules for the new blocks: an honour needs a real award record, a year or date and a source, a position only when ranked and a level only when certified; a press item needs a defined source; a reach figure needs its date; a horizon needs a source and an expected or announced date, and a build warning names horizons whose date has passed; `awarded` cannot be hand-written; a song's or album's prose may not carry a lyric block; a modern person's school or order needs two independent sources (the modern-figures rulings), and "made by Muslims" needs a basis for records in the browser's window.

- The Modern Islam Browser opens at /ar/modern/, /en/modern/ and /ur/modern/ over the 2,010 existing records whose dated facts fall from 1976 on: a home with the year-by-year ribbon (Hijri and Gregorian), the nineteen section tiles with their counts and newest records, the upcoming and new-in-30-days strips, the reach-index links, the countries and the languages; a page per section with facets by kind, decade and country (the figures section grouped by field) and a name filter that needs no JavaScript to read the full list; a page per year since 1976, per country and per language; the new-records page; the reach-index pages with their printed formula; a strip on every entity page in the window with its sections, honours, footprint, dated reach figures and upcoming badge, and the honoured records on award pages; a landing card and an Explore menu entry. Lists say "listed is not endorsed"; nothing is ranked by taste. A section of more than 300 records (the figures section lists 1,467 people) renders compact rows in every one of its lists, so the page stays under its 700 KB budget while the full list remains plain HTML. The pilot records for the sections still empty follow; nothing is live until the founder deploys.
- `docs/modern-islam/session-prompt.md`: the self-contained hand-off prompt for the sessions that continue the browser from Phase 3 (the state at hand-off, the pilot records still to enrich, the Mac-browser research toolkit including Apple Vision OCR and the microSD rule, the validator lessons of the pilot, and the cadence of one commit per batch and one merged pull request every 50 to 100 completed items).
- Every browser and explorer is now documented and exposed the same way (founder rule, 6 November 2026, written into `AGENTS.md`): a register, `docs/site/browsers.md`, lists the Time Browser, Time Machine, Hijri calendar, the landing explorers, the Screening Room, the Qur'an, hadith and poetry readers, the Fatwa and Khutab explorers, the Job Browser, the Inventions Browser, the Modern Islam Browser and Education with their routes, status, what each has today and what it will have; the plan index gains the missing rows for plans 22 to 25; the roadmap, the experience plan, the engineering set (requirements FR-15 and FR-16, design, implementation, extensibility recipe 2.7, the index), the executive deck, the root README, `docs/status.md` and the lanes registry (Time Browser and Fatwa rows) now name them. The static API gains one export per feature (`/api/v1/jobs.json`, `fatwa.json`, `khutab.json`, `poetry.json`, `screens.json`, `time-browser/index.json`), documented with the OpenAPI file and `llms.txt`, and every browser landing page exposes its title and lede to search so the feature is findable by name in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- A new Backstage page (`/ar/backstage/`, `/en/backstage/`, `/ur/backstage/`, linked from the footer) tells how the museum is made: the founder directing a fleet of AI agents with Muse as orchestrator, the lanes, the waves and the merge gates, and everything else the project produces — the 3:34 cinematic promo, the AI cartoon trailer in production, the comic on Webtoon, the fully AI-generated *Voices of the Museum* music suite, and the social videos. It ends with the agents' guest book: Muse's signed entry stands first, and each other fleet agent — Claude, Devin, Cursor, Copilot, Codex, ChatGPT, Antigravity, Grok — adds its own signed, dated entry when the founder asks it, under the append protocol in `docs/agents/backstage.md`; nobody writes in another agent's name.
- A Method page (`/{lang}/method/`, Arabic «المنهج») in Arabic, English and Urdu states the museum's working rules in the open, digested from its own documents: what it holds (the twenty-four classes, the nine wings, the span from the world of 610 to the present including the modern era); the publication standard (claims cite defined sources, provenance is recorded, one defined cited source suffices, no review stands between a record that meets the bar and publication; data CC0, prose CC BY-SA 4.0); how topics, people and works are chosen (gap-driven coverage with the gaps published, lanes and waves with nobody skipped, minimal honest records where evidence is thin, the modern-figures reach thresholds and Tier-A evidence for inventions); how classification is guarded (the five claim statuses, a person's faith only on two independent sources attesting it for the person himself — never office, headship, family, descent or an infobox — with doubt recorded as `unknown` and the contested reading preserved; views named before they are stated under the stated Sunni frame; unrecognised wings default to Ideas, multi-wing entries show every wing as a tag); what the museum refuses (unsourced claims, single-source sensitive classifications, depictions of prophets, sources that fail the authenticity gate, unverifiable or aggregator modern channels, anything beyond well-sourced public facts for the living, hidden gaps and silent skips); ages and sensitivity (kid mode, the children's wing's own rulebook); and corrections made in the open, with links to the governing documents. Linked from every page's footer beside the authenticity link.
- A dispute-resolution protocol now stands in `CONTRIBUTING.md` ("Disputes and contested claims"): a dispute is a pull request against the single record, settled by two independent sources attesting the value directly, with attested minority readings preserved in the record's note rather than deleted, structured-data values treated as leads rather than proof, escalation to a recorded founder ruling where the evidence standard cannot decide, and no drive-by reversals of settled values. Alongside it, `docs/agents/orchestration.md` documents how the agent fleet actually builds the museum — git as the only shared memory, bounded waves with file-level briefs and hard limits, the literal "0 errors" / "0 fail" merge gates, the founder as router and court of appeal, and the wave-brief template with the per-wave pipeline.

### Fixed
- **Site build restored.** The `llms.txt` page had a stray backtick pair inside its template string (the Tafsir Browser entry), which stopped the build at the page's compile step and failed the Validate and build run. The two terms are now written as plain words, and the wording of the entry is unchanged.
- **Tafsir volume pages build again.** The volume list was declared outside the page's `getStaticPaths` function, which Astro cannot read, so the `main` build failed with "volumes is not defined". The list is now built inside the function; the pages and their routes are unchanged.

- Modern Islam Browser, enterprise section, batch 05: six more Islamic banks with Arabic, English and Urdu prose — Gatehouse Bank (London), Bank Islam Brunei Darussalam, Pakistan's Faysal Bank (which lost its Shariah status in 2002 and regained it as a full Islamic bank in December 2022), Bangladesh's Al-Arafah Islami Bank and Social Islami Bank, and Turkey's state-owned Ziraat Katılım. - Modern Islam Browser, books section, batches 02–03: twelve more translations of the meanings of the Qur'an since 1976, each with Arabic, English and Urdu prose: T. B. Irving's First American Version (1985), Hamid Choi's Korean (1988/1997), Esad Duraković's Bosnian (2004), Laleh Bakhtiar's Sublime Quran (2007, Kazi), Tarif Khalidi's Penguin translation (2008), Bahauddeen Nadwi's Malayalam (2015), Assane Sylla's Wolof (2003), Maryati Sastrawijaya's Sundanese (2011, the only complete one by an Indonesian woman), the Turkmen Mufti Directorate's (2006), Sheikh Othman's Italian (2015), the Ahmadiyya Russian translation (2006, labelled as Ahmadiyya) and the Hebrew al-Muntakhab (2023). Sources: the Münster Global Qur'an reviews, the Mail & Guardian and The National. Content only, no deployment.

- tafsir: Jami' al-Bayan (Shakir) vol. 15 pp. 49-55 filed in ar, en and ur (Yunus 10:21-24, reports 17592-17598): the plot against the signs, the ship in the storm and sincere prayer, the transgression against themselves, and the start of the likeness of worldly life.

- tafsir: Jami' al-Bayan (Shakir) vol. 15 pp. 15-21 filed in ar, en and ur (Yunus 10:2-4, reports 17541-17552): the footing of truth, the readings of 'sihr/sahir', the six days and the Throne, and the return and repetition of creation.
- tafsir: Jami' al-Bayan (Shakir) vol. 15 filed in ar, en and ur, pp. 9-15 (Yunus 10:1-2, reports 17518-17541): the opening letters, 'these are the verses of the wise Book' and the start of 'a footing of truth'.

- Modern Islam Browser, enterprise section, batch 02: twelve finance records with Arabic, English and Urdu prose — seven new (Al Baraka Group, Al Rayan Bank UK, Bank of London and The Middle East, Bahrain Islamic Bank, Bank Albilad, Bank Muamalat Indonesia, and the standard-setters AAOIFI and IFSB) and four drafts enriched (Alinma, Jaiz, Sharjah Islamic Bank, Bank Muamalat Malaysia). - Modern Islam Browser, books section, batch 01: seven English translations of the meanings of the Qur'an published since 1976 now have full records with Arabic, English and Urdu prose. Muhammad Asad's *The Message of the Qur'an* (1980, with the 1964 partial edition and the Saudi Permanent Committee's position and the academic reading as labelled perspectives), Hilali–Khan (1977, every edition to the 1997 King Fahd Complex text), Saheeh International (1997), Taqi Usmani's *Meanings of the Noble Qur'an* (2007), *The Clear Quran* (2015/2016), *The Study Quran* (2015) and Abdel Haleem's Oxford translation (2004). Content comes from the University of Münster's Global Qur'an reviews, CNN and the publisher, with Wikipedia and Wikidata used only for entity facts. The Hilali–Khan draft carried the Wikidata item of Usmani's translation; that is corrected. Content only, no deployment.

- Modern Islam Browser, enterprise section, batch 01: eleven Islamic banks rewritten from thin drafts into full records with Arabic, English and Urdu prose — Alrajhi Bank, Dubai Islamic Bank, Meezan Bank, Islami Bank Bangladesh, Boubyan Bank, Faisal Islamic Bank of Egypt, Bank Islam Malaysia, Emirates Islamic, Bank Syariah Indonesia, Qatar Islamic Bank and Abu Dhabi Islamic Bank. Each now cites a Wikipedia article at its recorded revision, gives founding dates with Hijri equivalents, its Sharia basis and ownership, and Forbes Middle East 2025 rankings where the list was cited; lawsuits and disputes (the US 9/11 suits against Alrajhi, the Ryan Cornelius case, Islami Bank's 2022 loan crisis) are told with each side attributed. Unsourced superlatives from the drafts ("the world's first", "fastest-growing") were dropped and listed in the section's progress file. Content only, no deployment.

- Registered the Bibliotheca Alexandrina scan of Ibn Kathir's Tafsir (item AAlexandrina-024188, Dar al-Fikr printing) as its own source, `source/tafsir-ibn-kathir-alexandria-024188`, distinct from the Dar al-Andalus edition, so its printed pages (first use: p. 119 on 2:81) can now be cited. The library note for the Tabari harvest records the new source.
- The Modern Islam Browser's pilot batch, researched page by page in Chrome on the founder's Mac: Maher Zain's album Thank You Allah (2009) with its fifteen tracks, its Malaysian and Indonesian platinum certifications, its 2011 Malaysian music-award nomination and its video view counts as dated platform figures; Canadian Islamic History Month (October, since 2007) from the Government of Canada's page; the United Nations' International Day to Combat Islamophobia (15 March, resolution of 2022) from the UN's own page; the 2034 FIFA World Cup in Saudi Arabia as an upcoming record with the host's position and the rights criticism side by side; the Museum of Islamic Art in Doha (2008); five award records (the King Faisal Prize, the Nobel Prize, the African Footballer of the Year, platinum album certifications, Anugerah Industri Muzik); and the first honours and sports blocks on existing people: Mahathir Mohamad's King Faisal Prize of 1997, Ahmed Zewail's Nobel Prize of 1999 and King Faisal Prize of 1989, and Mohamed Salah's football record with his CAF awards of 2017 and 2018. Every section of the browser now shows at least one record. Two leads were held back with their reasons and overrides in the lane's progress file (the Islamic Arts Biennale, which has no defined source yet, and the 2007 instrument behind the Canadian month).
- Modern Islam Browser, pilot batch 2 (content only, no deployment): three thin pilot records completed from Wikipedia, Wikidata, the sites themselves and the Wayback Machine, read page by page in Chrome. IslamQA (web) now carries its 1996 start and the 1997 alternative side by side, its earliest archived capture (19 January 1998, as islam-qa.com), its sixteen languages, three dated Similarweb visit figures and the 2010 block in Saudi Arabia, attributed; an unsourced line naming Salman al-Ouda as a founder was dropped. *The Message* (1976, film) gains its London premiere date, the runtimes of its Arabic and English versions, nine sourced reception entries (the al-Azhar approval and its withdrawal, the 1974 halt of filming in Morocco, the bans until 2018, the 1977 Washington siege, the 2007 Indian dub and the 2008 Hyderabad stoppage, the 2017 restoration, the critics, the unmade remake) and its 1978 Oscar nomination for Maurice Jarre's score. *Omar* (2012, series) gains its Ramadan 1433 first broadcast, production facts, the scholars' committee, its broadcasts in twelve countries and a perspectives block with both Sunni positions on portraying the Companions first, then the labelled Shia criticism. The Bishwa Ijtema (conferences) gains its history from the first 1946 gathering at Kakrail Mosque to the move to Tongi in 1967, how the three days and the final supplication run, the two-phase system since 2010, and seven dated editions with press attendance estimates marked as estimates (2 million in 2001, 4 million in 2023), the 2008 weather curtailment, the 2024 deaths and the December 2024 clashes between two Tablighi factions. One new award record, the Academy Awards. Ten source records, fifteen prose files in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Modern Islam Browser, pilot batch 3 (content only, no deployment): *al-Raheeq al-Makhtum* (books) is now a full record: how it came first of 171 studies in the Muslim World League's sira competition (announced 1396 AH, results Shaban 1398, prizes in Mecca 1399/1979), its four parts, three editions with ISBNs, its English and Bengali translations and its author's abridgement, with the competition written as its own award record. Islamic Relief Worldwide (organisations) is written as a group from its 1984 founding in Birmingham to the September 2026 expulsion of Islamic Relief USA, with its structure, milestones, awards and the allegations against it each set beside the charity's reply and the audits' findings; the older draft card `listing/islamic-relief` is merged into it as an alias, so existing links still resolve. Iqraa TV (media) is new: launched 21 October 1998 within ART, its programmes and presenters, its English service from 2011 and the end of its satellite broadcasts on 1 January 2025. Five source records, three stubs files, twelve prose files in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Modern Islam Browser, pilot batch 4 (content only, no deployment): Mohamed Salah gains his Wikipedia footprint (about a hundred languages) and a dated Instagram figure read on his profile (67.8 million followers, 6 November 2026). The Dubai International Holy Quran Award is rewritten as a competition: its 1997 launch with the 1998 alternative, its branches, the international and women's winners from 2017 to 2024 and the 2022 anniversary stamp; its Islamic Personality of the Year becomes an award record, and nine laureates already in the museum (al-Sha'rawi, Nadwi, Zayed bin Sultan, Izetbegović, al-Sudais, El-Naggar, Zakir Naik, Ahmed el-Tayeb, King Salman) now carry it as an honour. The Sheikh Zayed Grand Mosque (built) gets its 1994–2007 construction, architect and designers, models, contractors, dimensions, capacity and minaret height with the two published figures side by side, materials, carpet, chandeliers and calligraphers. The planned merge of `building/sheikh-zayed-al-nahyan-mosque` into it was stopped: that card's Wikidata item is a different mosque, in North Gaza, destroyed in 2023. Four source records, one award record, nine prose files.
- Modern Islam Browser, pilot batch 5 (content only, no deployment): the last two pilot records. Kuwait Finance House (enterprise) gains its history from its founding in 1977 as Kuwait's first Islamic bank and its first day of business on 31 August 1978, its expansion to Turkey, Bahrain, Malaysia (and the announced exit), Saudi Arabia, the UAE, Iraq and Egypt, the 2022 Ahli United Bank acquisition, its state-fund ownership, dated 2025 figures, and two Forbes Middle East rankings recorded through a new ranking award record. The International Islamic University Malaysia (learning) gains its 1982 origin with Mahathir Mohamad and the OIC, its establishment on 23 May 1983, its first 153 students and first graduates of 1987, fourteen faculties, the usrah requirement, its achievements and today's leadership. Mahathir Mohamad's record gains its Wikipedia footprint (143 languages). The pilot is complete but for one dated reach figure for Mahathir Mohamad. Two source records, one award record, nine prose files.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on 4:128 continues from printed p. 274 at report 10599 and is filed through report 10618 (pp. 274-280), in Arabic, English and Urdu, citing volume 9 page by page. The first clause of 4:128 closes (the Rafi' ibn Khadij story, the Sawda report, the reading of the settlement verb) and the second clause on the soul's readiness for avarice opens; the next report is 10619 on p. 281.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on 4:128 closes (reports 10619 to 10625: the wife's avarice for her share of days and maintenance, the Sawda report, and the meaning of avarice), and the commentary on 4:129 opens on the unequal love between wives, filed through report 10642 (pp. 281-287) in Arabic, English and Urdu; the next report is 10643 on p. 287.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on 4:129 closes (reports 10643 to 10671 on the forbidden inclination, the 'hanging' wife and the reform and forgiveness of the verse), and the commentary on 4:130 opens on separation and God's bounty, filed through report 10673 (pp. 288-294) in Arabic, English and Urdu; the 4:130 passage files are created for this batch. The next report is 10674.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 1 (content only, no deployment): six Egyptian Qur'an reciters join the new "Reciters, muezzins and munshids" field, each with a full life in Arabic, English and Urdu. Mohamed Mahmoud al-Tablawi, who led the reciters' syndicate from 2011 until he died in Ramadan 2020. Mahmud Ali al-Banna, a radio voice from 1948 and the syndicate's first deputy head. Ahmad Nuaina, the physician-reciter appointed Egypt's Chief of Quran Recitations in 2025. Mahmoud al-Shahat Anwar, of the younger generation. Raghib Mustafa Ghalwash, who gave the adhan at al-Hussein Mosque as a conscript before joining the radio in 1962. Muhammad Jebril, the tarawih imam of the Mosque of Amr ibn al-As, whose 2015 dispute with the Ministry of Awqaf is told with both the ministry's and the court's positions. Every fact cites the Wikipedia or Wikidata revision it was read from; leads that could not be confirmed are listed with the reason.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 2 (content only, no deployment): five reciters beyond Egypt, each with a full life in Arabic, English and Urdu. Fares Abbad of Sanaa, now an imam in Dhahran. Ibrahim al-Akhdar of Medina, imam of both Holy Mosques and teacher of the readings. Kâni Karaca, the blind hafiz and mevlid singer called the last voice of Istanbul's recitation tradition. Aziz Alili, muezzin of Sarajevo's Emperor's Mosque in the 1980s and now head of the Islamic Community in Zagreb. Maria Ulfah, who in 1980 became the first woman to win an international recitation award in Malaysia.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 3 (content only, no deployment): the first muezzins of the Masjid al-Haram enter the museum, with full lives in Arabic, English and Urdu. Ali Ahmad Mulla is the head of its muezzins, from a family that has given the call there for generations, and he gave the first sunset call after the 1979 siege. Farouk Hadrawi is the Haram's muezzin and the muezzin of Namira at Arafat. Ibrahim al-Madani, the Haram's voice of the dawn call, died in September 2026. Three reciters join them: Omar al-Qazabri, imam of Casablanca's Hassan II Mosque; al-Zain Muhammad Ahmad of Khartoum's Sayyida Sanhouri Mosque; and Abdelaziz al-Karaouri of Casablanca. Where the sources disagree on a date, both readings are given.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 4 (content only, no deployment): nine munshids and nasheed singers, each with a full life in Arabic, English and Urdu. From Egypt come Sayed al-Naqshabandi, whose supplications became part of the Ramadan iftar on the radio; Nasr al-Din Tubar, who passed the radio test at the seventh try; Yasin al-Tuhami; Ahmad Barrin, the blind praise singer of Upper Egypt; and the singer-songwriter Hamza Namira. Syria gives Abu Ratib, Sabri Mudallal (muezzin of the Great Mosque of Aleppo) and Hamza Shakkur, who founded Ensemble Al-Kindi. From Pakistan comes the naat reciter Khursheed Ahmad. A new award record, Pakistan's Pride of Performance, carries Khursheed Ahmad's 1996 honour.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 5 (content only, no deployment): five Sufi singers and musicians, each with a full life in Arabic, English and Urdu. The Pakistani qawwal Aziz Mian holds the record for the longest released qawwali, at about 115 minutes. Munshi Raziuddin of the Delhi gharana leads to his son Fareed Ayaz, who now leads Pakistan's best-known qawwali party with his brother Abu Muhammad. Badar Miandad was a cousin of Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan. Kudsi Erguner is the Turkish master of the Mevlevi ney. Three of them join Khursheed Ahmad on the Pride of Performance page, each with the year they received it.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 6 (content only, no deployment): four great names of qawwali and Sufi song are rebuilt from bare drafts into full lives in Arabic, English and Urdu, every fact cited to the article revision it was read from. They are Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan, his nephew Rahat Fateh Ali Khan, Abida Parveen and the Sabri Brothers. Three errors are corrected along the way: Abida Parveen's Arabic name was misspelled; the Sabri Brothers were founded in 1956, not 1958, which was the year of their first record; and their group ended in 2021. Their Pride of Performance years (1978, 1984, 1987) are now on the award's page.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 7 (content only, no deployment): six award records open in Arabic, English and Urdu: Pakistan's Nishan-e-Imtiaz, Hilal-e-Imtiaz and Sitara-e-Imtiaz, Egypt's Order of the Republic and Order of Sciences and Arts, and Japan's Fukuoka Prize. Honours that were only told in the prose now appear on the records. Abida Parveen carries all three Pakistani orders, Nusrat Fateh Ali Khan the 1996 Fukuoka Prize, Sayed al-Naqshabandi the Order of the Republic (1989), and Mahmud Ali al-Banna the Order of Sciences and Arts (1990).
- Modern Islam Browser, King Faisal Prize: 29 laureates who already had a page in the museum now carry the prize as a dated honour, from Abul A'la Maududi, its first laureate in 1979, to Ali Hassan Mwinyi in 2022. Each records its category: 23 for Service to Islam, five for Islamic Studies (among them Fuat Sezgin and al-Albani) and one for Arabic Literature (Yahya Haqqi). The prize's own page now lists them. The 1990 laureate Khurshid Ahmad, an economist, is not confused with the naat reciter of the same name.
- Modern Islam Browser, figures wave 1, batch 9 (content only, no deployment): six King Faisal Prize laureates who had no page yet, in Arabic, English and Urdu. Ali al-Tantawi, the Syrian judge and broadcaster (Service to Islam, 1990); Sayyid Sabiq, author of 'Fiqh al-Sunna' (Islamic Studies, 1994); Aisha Abd al-Rahman (Bint al-Shati) (Arabic Literature, 1994); Mohammad Natsir, Indonesia's fifth prime minister (Service to Islam, 1980); Mahmud Muhammad Shakir, editor of al-Tabari's Tafsir (Arabic Literature, 1984); and the hadith scholar Muhammad Mustafa al-Azami (Islamic Studies, 1980). Where the sources disagree, as on Natsir's death date and al-Azami's birth year, both readings are kept.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 131 to 135 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 295 to p. 301 (reports 10674 to 10677): God's dominion and the charge to the earlier and later people; the keeping and direction of God; the warning against replacement and betrayal, with the Salman report; the hypocrites who want the reward of this world; and the start of the command to stand firm for justice as witnesses for God.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 135 of sura 4 continues from printed p. 302 to p. 307 (reports 10678 to 10685): standing firm for justice in testimony even against oneself and kin; the Suddi report of the rich and poor disputants; the early Muslims' practice in a father's testimony for kin; the Qatada and Moses reports on justice; and the reading of 'twisting' and 'turning away' as addressed to judges or to witnesses. Filed in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 135 of sura 4 closes at printed p. 312 (reports 10686 to 10696 on twisting and turning away from testimony, the readings of 'lawa', and the closing words on God's awareness), and the commentary on verses 136 and 137 is filed from p. 312 to p. 314 (belief in God, His Messenger and both Books; the apostates who believe, disbelieve and increase in disbelief), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 137 to 140 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 314 to p. 321 (reports 10697 to 10710): the apostates who believe, disbelieve and increase in disbelief, with the reports on the repentance of an apostate; the announcement of painful punishment to the hypocrites; might belonging to God; and the prohibition on sitting with people of falsehood while they engage in it, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 140 of sura 4 closes at printed p. 322 (the hypocrites and unbelievers gathered in hell, and the readings of 'it has been revealed to you'), and verse 141 is filed from p. 323 to p. 328 (reports 10711 to 10720: the hypocrites who wait on the believers for spoils, the promise that God gives the unbelievers no path over them, and the reports from Ali and Ibn Abbas that this refers to the Day of Resurrection), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 142 and 143 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 329 to p. 335 (reports 10721 to 10736): the hypocrites' deception of God and the light taken from them on the Day of Resurrection; their lazy prayer and little remembrance, as show; and the wavering between belief and unbelief, with the goat and the river parables, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 144 to 147 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 336 to p. 342 (reports 10737 to 10747): the prohibition on taking unbelievers as allies and the 'clear authority' as proof; the lowest rank of the fire and its coffins; the repentance of the hypocrites who reform and hold fast to God, with Hudhayfa's report; and the opening of verse 147 on what God gains from punishment, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 147 of sura 4 closes (report 10748, that God does not punish the thankful) and verse 148 is filed from printed p. 343 to p. 349 (reports 10749 to 10764): the wronged person's right to speak of his wrong, the readings of the vowel, and the hospitality reports, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 148 to 153 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 350 to p. 356 (reports 10765 to 10769): the wronged person's right to speak of his wrong, the pardon of evil and the good shown, the Jews and Christians who separate God's messengers, the believers who do not separate them, and the request for a book from heaven, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 153 to 155 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 356 to p. 363 (reports 10770 to 10773): the request for a book from heaven and the rebuke of those who asked Moses to show them God openly, the calf and the pardon through repentance, the Mount, the gate and the Sabbath, and the breaking of the covenant with the claim that their hearts are wrapped, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 155 to 157 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 363 to p. 370 (reports 10774 to 10782): the sealing of hearts and the curse for the breaking of the covenant, the slander against Mary, and the reports on the likeness cast upon another in place of Jesus, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 157 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 371 to p. 377 (reports 10783 to 10792): the reports on the likeness cast upon a disciple in place of Jesus, the Ibn Ishaq account of the disciples and Judas, the preferred view of Wahb's first account, and the closing words on those who doubted, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 158 of sura 4 (God raised Jesus; the Mighty and Wise) and verse 159 is filed from printed p. 378 to p. 384 (reports 10793 to 10820): the readings of the People of the Book believing in Jesus before their death, read as before Jesus' own death or at the moment of death, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 159 of sura 4 closes at printed p. 390 (reports 10821 to 10832: the People of the Book believing in Jesus before Jesus' own death, with the Abu Hurayra report on his descent), and verse 160 is filed from p. 390 to p. 391 (reports 10833 to 10835: the forbidden good things, hindering from God's path, and usury), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 160 to 162 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 391 to p. 398 (reports 10836 to 10838): the forbidden usury, bribes and the price of books; the painful punishment for the unbelievers among them; and the firm in knowledge among the People of the Book, with the grammatical and scribal discussion of 'those who establish prayer', in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 162 of sura 4 closes at p. 399, and verse 163 is filed from p. 399 to p. 405 (reports 10839 to 10844): the revelation to Muhammad as to Noah and the prophets, the reports on the Jews' claim that God sent nothing after Moses, the readings of the Psalms, and God's speech to Moses, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 163 to 170 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 405 to p. 412 (reports 10845 to 10852): the reports on God's speech to Moses, the messengers as good news and warning so that no argument remains, the witness of God and the angels to Muhammad's prophethood, the barring from God's way, the eternal punishment of those who disbelieve and do wrong, and the call to believe, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 170 of sura 4 closes at p. 413 with the grammatical views on 'better for you', and verse 171 is filed from p. 415 to p. 419 (report 10853 to 10854): exaggeration in religion, Jesus as God's messenger and word, the meaning of 'Messiah', and the spirit from God, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 171 of sura 4 closes at p. 424 (the views on 'a spirit from Him', the Trinity, God's oneness, and Jesus as a servant), verse 172 is filed from p. 424 to p. 425 (reports 10856 to 10857), and verse 173 is begun at p. 426, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verses 173 to 176 of sura 4 is filed from printed p. 426 to p. 431 (reports 10858 to 10865): the punishment of those who disdain, the proof and the clear light as the Qur'an, those who hold fast to God, and the start of the kalala ruling with Abu Bakr's account of the inheritance verses, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 176 of sura 4 continues from report 10871 to report 10877 at p. 437: the last verses revealed, the place and occasion of the kalala verse with Hudhayfa's account, and Umar's varying statements on the kalala, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 176 of sura 4 (the kalala) is filed to the foot of p. 444 (reports 10878 to 10890): Umar's istikhara and the erased letter, his reported wish for the Prophet to have explained the kalala, the reports of the Prophet's answer, and the reasoning on a sister's share with a daughter, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 176 of sura 4 closes at p. 446 with the end of sura 4, and the commentary on sura al-Ma'ida verse 1 opens at p. 449 and is filed to p. 451 (reports 10891 to 10904): God making things clear lest people go astray in inheritance, and the meaning of 'covenants' as pledges, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on sura al-Ma'ida verse 1 is filed from printed p. 452 to p. 458 (reports 10905 to 10934): the covenants as pledges and pre-Islamic alliances, the five kinds of contract, the beast of cattle with its fetuses, and the exception of carrion, blood and pig's flesh, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on sura al-Ma'ida verse 1 closes at p. 462 (the exception of game during pilgrimage and God's decree), and verse 2 is filed from p. 462 to p. 464 (reports 10938 to 10944) on the symbols of God, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on sura al-Ma'ida verse 2 is filed from printed p. 465 to p. 471 (reports 10945 to 10957): God's symbols, the sacred month, the offerings and the garlands of the sanctuary's bark, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on sura al-Ma'ida verse 2 closes with the account of al-Hutam and the abrogation reports (reports 10958 to 10977), filed from printed p. 472 to p. 478, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on sura al-Ma'ida verse 2 is filed from printed p. 478 to p. 485 (reports 10978 to 10992): the abrogation of the sacred-month clause, seeking bounty in pilgrimage, the permission to hunt after leaving the state of pilgrimage, and the hatred that must not move believers, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on sura al-Ma'ida verse 2 closes at p. 491 (the hatred that must not move believers, the reading of 'an' and 'in', the abrogation question, and helping one another in righteousness), and verse 3 is begun at p. 492 (the carrion), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida is filed from printed p. 493 to p. 499 (reports 11002 to 11019): the swine, what was dedicated to other than God, the strangled, the beaten to death, the fallen, and the gored, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 499 to p. 506 (reports 11021 to 11046): the gored, the prey's kill, the exception of what is slaughtered while still alive, and Malik's view on animals torn by beasts, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 507 to p. 513 (reports 11047 to 11071): why the list of forbidden dead animals is repeated, the stone altars of the Age of Ignorance, and the divining arrows, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 514 to p. 520 (reports 11072 to 11087): Ibn Ishaq's account of Hubal and its arrows, the clauses on transgression, the despair of the disbelievers and 'do not fear them', and the first views on the perfecting of the religion, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 520 to p. 527 (reports 11088 to 11101): his preference on the perfecting of the religion, the completion of the favour, the approval of Islam as the religion, and the reports that the verse came down at Arafa on a Friday, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 527 to p. 534 (reports 11101 to 11118): the remaining reports on the day the verse came down (Arafa, a Friday, against a Monday or an unknown day) and the start of the exception for a person driven by hunger, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 535 to p. 541 (reports 11119 to 11131): 'not inclining to sin', 'God is forgiving, merciful', and the first reports on how much a person in necessity may eat of what is forbidden, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 3 of sura al-Ma'ida is closed at printed p. 542 (reports 11132 and 11133, the readings of the word for gathering vegetables), and verse 4 is begun through p. 548 (reports 11134 to 11150): what is lawful, the trained hunting animals (jawarih), the occasion about the killing of dogs, and the first views on which animals count, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Modern Islam Browser, music section batch 1 (content only, no deployment), researched page by page in Chrome on the founder's Mac: five recordings with their full track lists and sourced honours, in Arabic, English and Urdu. Sami Yusuf's *My Ummah* (2005), with the two sales estimates and The Guardian's lower figures kept side by side; Maher Zain's *Forgive Me* (2012) with its Malaysian platinum certification (seven times by one article, four by the other, both kept) and its videos; Youssou N'Dour's *Egypt* (2004), the Wolof album in praise of the Senegalese Sufi masters, with its Grammy, its chart peaks and the Senegalese boycott and Egyptian ban told with N'Dour's own answer; Yusuf's *An Other Cup* (2006), his return to Western pop; and Atif Aslam's 2015 Coke Studio rendition of the qawwali *Tajdar-e-Haram*, Pakistan's first music video to pass 100 million YouTube views. The Awakening Music label record is rewritten from its one-line draft as a `record-label` with its founders, ownership, releases and history from The Guardian (2012). Three new award records: the Grammy Awards (with the contemporary world music category), the UK Albums Chart and Billboard World Albums. Eighteen source records. No lyrics, no players.
- Modern Islam Browser, web section, batch 1 (content only, no deployment): the first apps join the section of websites, apps, games and devices, each with a full account in Arabic, English and Urdu. Nusuk, the Saudi Ministry of Hajj and Umrah's platform for Umrah permits, Rawda visits, Hajj registration and Umrah visas since September 2022, with the download and user figures the ministry announced. Minhaj, the Muslim World League's app launched in Mecca in November 2025 after the Islamic Fiqh Council approved the idea. Muzz, the Muslim marriage app founded in London in 2015 as Muzmatch and renamed after a trademark case. SalamWeb, the Malaysian browser of 2019 that filtered content and carried prayer tools, now discontinued. Muslim Pro, the prayer-times app of 2010, told with its own history and with the 2020 Motherboard investigation into its sharing of location data. Every store figure is dated and read on the store page; two older draft claims that the makers are Muslims were withdrawn because no source attests them, and an Islamic State children's app was left out as propaganda.
- Modern Islam Browser, web section, batch 2 (content only, no deployment): three more apps and programs in Arabic, English and Urdu. IslamicFinder, online by December 1998 as a directory of US mosques with local prayer times and run in 2001 by the non-profit Wasat.com, with its Athan app today. Tarteel, the app that listens to Qur'an recitation and corrects mistakes, which joined MIT's startup accelerator in 2019 and launched on phones in January 2020. Sabily, the Ubuntu-based Linux distribution for Muslim users released from 2007 to 2011 with prayer-time, Qur'an and zakat programs. Store figures are dated; a news profile that met a bot check was left unread and is listed as a lead.
- Modern Islam Browser, web section, batch 3 (content only, no deployment): the first games, in Arabic, English and Urdu. Islamic Fun, a 1999 British quiz game for Muslim children whose minigame "The Resistance" stirred a press controversy in 2002, told with its makers' and its critics' words side by side. Quraish, the Syrian real-time strategy game of 2005 set in the early battles of Islam. Unearthed: Trail of Ibn Battuta, the Saudi adventure game of 2013 that follows the traveller's trail and was badly received. Two contested Syrian shooters and several studios were held back, with the reasons listed for the founder.
- Modern Islam Browser, web section, batch 4 (content only, no deployment): five famous Islamic websites in Arabic, English and Urdu. IslamOnline, launched in 1997 with Yusuf al-Qaradawi among its founders, whose 2010 crisis between its Cairo staff and its Qatari owners is told from both sides' own words. Askimam, the South African mufti Ebrahim Desai's Hanafi fatwa site of 2000. Darulifta-Deoband.com, Darul Uloom Deoband's Urdu and English fatwa site of 2007, with India's 2022 child-rights complaint, the seminary's reply and the block that followed. Halalgoogling, the filtered search engine of 2013. Islamweb, Qatar's ministry portal of 1998, with the figures it reports.
- Modern Islam Browser, web section, batch 5 (content only, no deployment): Islam.ru, the Russian-language portal founded in Dagestan in 2001; Islamopedia Online, Harvard's academic database of religious opinions (2007); and the Sahih al-Bukhari Encyclopedia, the online library devoted to al-Bukhari's collection, its manuscripts and commentaries, first released in 2016 and linked to the museum's record of the Sahih.
- Modern Islam Browser, web section, batch 6 (content only, no deployment): two online libraries, a hadith site and the first device, in Arabic, English and Urdu. The Quranic Arabic Corpus of the University of Leeds (2009), which tags every word of the Qur'an by traditional grammar; al-Maktaba al-Shamela, the free library program of some eight thousand books first released in 2005; Sunnah.com, told from its own account of its sources and numbering; and the digital Qur'an players that spread in Indonesia, as Reuters described them in 2007.

- Modern Islam Browser, music section batch 2 (content only, no deployment): Sami Yusuf's *Without You* (2009), the album the artist disowned, with his statement and his label's reply side by side; Maher Zain's *One* (2016) in its English, Arabic and Turkish editions; Yusuf Islam's children's alphabet album *A Is for Allah* (2000), whose Qur'an recitations are linked, never typed; Raihan's debut *Puji-Pujian* (1996/1997). The Malaysian nasheed groups Raihan and Rabbani and the American group Native Deen now have full, sourced records (Raihan's founding year corrected from 1993 to 1996; an invented album title removed; Native Deen's unsourced founding year dropped), and the Anugerah Industri Muzik award record gains its organiser, its first ceremony of 15 December 1993 and its nasyid categories. The 3D tour's Raihan and Native Deen stops cite the records' new sources. Seventeen source records.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 4 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 549 to p. 555 (reports 11151 to 11166): which animals count as trained hunters (dogs only, or also hawks and cheetahs), the hadith of Adi ibn Hatim on the hawk, the meaning of 'you teach them of what God taught you', and the first reports on a trained dog that eats from its catch, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 4 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 556 to p. 562 (reports 11167 to 11201): the four views on whether game eaten from by a hunting dog or hawk may be eaten (never, only for dogs, only if a third is left, always), with the reports from Ibn Abbas, al-Sha'bi, Salman, Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas and Abu Hurayra, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 4 of sura al-Ma'ida continues from printed p. 563 to p. 570 (reports 11201 to 11217): his preferred reading of what training a hunting animal means, the sound hadith of Adi ibn Hatim against the Salman report, the two views on 'eat of what they hold for you', and the grammarians on the word 'min', in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Al-Tabari's commentary on verse 4 of sura al-Ma'ida is closed at printed p. 572 (the mention of God's name over the catch and 'fear God') and verse 5 is begun through p. 577 (reports 11218 to 11240): the food of the People of the Book, the dispute over the slaughter of the Christians of Banu Taghlib, al-Shafi'i's view and al-Tabari's reply, in Arabic, English and Urdu.

- Modern Islam Browser, music section batch 3 (content only, no deployment): three groups and two Sufi film songs, in Arabic, English and Urdu. The Danish hip-hop group Outlandish (1997), with its two disputes in the Danish press told with each side's words; the British Islamic hip-hop and nasheed group Mecca2Medina (1996) and its community work as The Salam Project; the British duo Poetic Pilgrimage (2002), subject of Al Jazeera's *Hip Hop Hijabis*; A. R. Rahman's qawwali *Kun Faya Kun* (Rockstar, 2011), dedicated to Nizamuddin Auliya, its Qur'anic title kept in transliteration only; and his *Khwaja Mere Khwaja* (2005, released on the Jodhaa Akbar soundtrack, 2008), composed for Mu'in al-Din Chishti. Ten source records. No lyrics.

### Changed
- The Modern Islam Browser now names the voices of worship and devotional song (founder's request, 6 November 2026): four person roles, `reciter`, `muezzin`, `munshid` and `sufi-singer`, join the role list, and the Figures section gains a field, "Reciters, muezzins and munshids", listed first so that a reciter who also preaches is filed with the reciters. The 48 person records that already carry one of these roles are gathered there at once (those with a dated fact from 1976 on appear in the browser). Three audio genres are added for recordings: Qur'an recitation and the call to prayer, each labelled "not music" in all three languages, and Sufi inshad and hadra. The operative prompt and plan 26 say how to write these records (riwaya, the muezzin's mosque and years, Qur'an text never typed, a Sufi repertoire never taken as a faith attestation), and the seeds list several hundred named leads with their generators.
- Ibn Zuhr's documented Muslim basis and explicitly qualified Sunni museum frame now appear in his identity fields as well as the invention narrative; the older unclassified entry and source disagreements remain visible, without inferring personal sect from legal study.
- The claims register and the DPGA evidence map record the twenty-fourth class and the new API routes as build outputs that are not live until deployment.

### Fixed
- White-paper audit remediation: the three MAJOR errors found by the phase-1 audit of the academic white paper (50 records, 269 claims re-checked against their own cited sources) are corrected. The Saadah Mosque card no longer places the mosque in Gurun, Kedah — its own linked Wikidata item (Q12497333) is the Saadah Mosque in West Sumatra, Indonesia, and the label and summaries now say so. Majorca's structured summary no longer carries al-Idrisi's description of Ibiza (many vineyards, a small fine town, between Denia and Minorca); it now matches the record's own prose account of Majorca from the same cited page — east of Ibiza, with a large city, a ruler and a garrison. The question "Who was Nuh?" no longer rests its central claims (the 950-year call, first of the Messengers of Steadfast Resolve, the story's place in the Qur'an) on a generic Wikidata citation that supports none of them; they are now cited to the Qur'an text (Tanzil) in the Arabic and English editions, with the Urdu edition's citation token to follow in the Urdu lane.
- The 3D tour's Sabri Brothers stop now lists the same two sources as its rebuilt record (English Wikipedia and Wikidata at their recorded revisions); it still named the old generic Wikidata source and turned `npm test` red on `main`.
- The 3D tour's Sabri Brothers stop lists the same sources as its record again (the English Wikipedia and Wikidata records the figures batch 06 gave it); the mismatch had turned `main` red on the tour test.
- Arabic and Urdu parentheses finished: the last unisolated values (award labels, journey and question summaries, and multi-line parentheticals) are wrapped, and the validator now rejects any new Arabic-script parenthesis without the isolate marks.
- Repaired 26 journey and related files whose inline Arabic and Urdu keys had lost their quotes in an earlier isolation pass; the waypoint titles and narratives are readable by the validator again.
- Fixed three journey files whose Arabic flow-map values had been broken by an earlier isolation pass (YAML would not parse); their parentheses are now isolated and the files parse again.

- Arabic parentheses: every Arabic-script parenthetical must now be wrapped in Unicode isolate marks (U+2066 ... U+2069), so brackets stay in the right place in right-to-left text. The rule is in AGENTS.md and applies to every agent and session, including harvesting, ingestion and OCR.
- The footer version now appears on UAT. Since PR #4392 the version is not baked into the pages: `shell.js` fills it in at run time by fetching `/api/v1/meta/deploy.json`, which the site Worker redirects to the environment's API host — `uat-api.islam.museum` on UAT. The Content Security Policy in `site/public/_headers` allowed only the production host in `connect-src`, so UAT browsers blocked the fetch and the footer kept showing the bare changelog link. The policy now allows both API hosts, a test pins them so a later edit cannot drop one, and the deployment doc records the rule. Production was not affected: its API host was already allowed.
- The Modern Islam Browser no longer links to year pages it never builds. Year pages exist from 1976 to the build year, but the "by decade" chips on a section page pointed at the decade's first year (1970, 2030), and the year chip on an entity page pointed at the record's own year, even a future one such as 2034 for the 2034 World Cup. The internal link check failed on all 33 of them in the three languages, so no pull request could pass. Now one helper decides: a year with a page is a link, a decade chip links to its first year that has a page (the 1970s to 1976), and any other year is shown as plain text. A unit test covers the helper, and the site check now fails the build on any link to a year page that does not exist.
- Event pages now publish complete structured data: each event, period and journey entry's machine-readable record includes the place the graph ties it to and the full date range the record carries (journeys take theirs from their legs), and practices are described as defined terms rather than events. Search engines had flagged the museum's event entries for missing locations and start dates that were present in the data all along, only stored in class-specific shapes.
