## v1.2.0 — 2026-11-05

### Added
- Taqi al-Din's six-cylinder pump design, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu with original maker attribution and decimal-table contribution preserved. The account separates a phased-pumping design from unproved historical manufacture, assumed modern models from original dimensions, and smoother delivery from constant-flow certification. An inspected licensed modern cam diagram illustrates only the principle, not the historical pump. Content only, no deployment.
- Ibn Munim's combinatorial enumeration, with full Arabic/English/Urdu invention, maker and work accounts. Selection, order and repetition stay distinct; al-Khalil and al-Karaji remain predecessors, while tentative composition, later copying and modern editions are not collapsed. Independently checked modern counting examples and an inspected CC0 triangle explain the ideas without claiming manuscript transcription or direct transmission to Pascal. Content only, no deployment.
- Al-Kashi's iterative one-degree sine computation, with full Arabic/English/Urdu invention and work accounts and preserved maker biography. The account separates inherited trigonometry from numerical improvement, conflicting completion/commentary reports from joint authorship, and a checked modern cubic illustration from an unread historical algorithm. The reused public-domain triangle is modern context, not a one-degree drawing. Content only, no deployment.
- Al-Kashi's high-precision circumference computation, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu with original maker attribution preserved. The1424 improvement is not discovery of pi or decimals; twice pi, numeral systems, rounding and modern polygon bounds remain distinct. The inspected public-domain hexagon image is modern context, not a manuscript or full historical calculation. Content only, no deployment.
- Ibn al-Baytar's comparative materia-medica method, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu with original maker/work biographies and attribution preserved. The account separates substantive materials from synonym entries, reported verification aims from modern medical proof, and a later public-domain botanical plate from medieval evidence. Earlier authors and Persian-name errors remain visible; no remedies, doses or deployment.
- Ibn Yunus's contribution to Cairo's solar-time tables, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu with original maker biography and attribution preserved. The surviving corpus's later additions are not all assigned to him; solar-time tables, the Hakimi Zij and separate lunar tables remain distinct. A modern paper calculation explains two-input spherical geometry, not manuscript cells, a prayer timetable or a pendulum clock. Licensed modern image is context only; no deployment.
- Habash al-Hasib's numerical shadow tables, with full Arabic/English/Urdu invention, maker and work accounts. Older shadow geometry, attributed table priority, uncertain chronology and differing manuscript/title interpretations remain distinct. Paper examples explain angle conventions and nonlinear ratios, not historical numeric cells or a solar-observation activity. Original maker attribution is preserved; the public-domain modern triangle is context only. Content only, no deployment.
- Abu Kamil's algebra with irrational coefficients as well as solutions, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. The account distinguishes inherited algebra from a specific extension and inspected modern scholarly notation from an unread manuscript. Original maker biography and attribution are preserved; uncertain life dates, reported reception and the modern public-domain triangle's contextual role stay explicit. Content only, no deployment.
- The plan for the Modern Islam Browser (plan 26 with the companion folder `docs/modern-islam/`): the museum's entrance to the last fifty years of Islam and Muslims, from 1 Muharram 1396 (3 January 1976) to today and into the upcoming, in nineteen sections (websites and apps, music, films, series, conferences, heritage months and festivals, rights and protest, books, upcoming, figures, organisations, media, sport, awards and competitions, achievements, enterprise, arts, built since 1976, learning) as views over the one graph. The plan sets a pilot-first order (one sourced record per section, the browser pages and a landing card in production before the waves), a browser-first research rule on the founder's Mac, the proposed `award` class and honours, footprint, reach and horizon fields, transparent reach indices instead of taste rankings, a seed programme for the first 10,000 to 50,000 subjects, and the operative prompt for any agent. Proposal only: nothing in schema, data, site or i18n changes until the founder approves it; a planning-only lane row is registered.
- Taqi al-Din's decimal trigonometric-table application, with full Arabic/English/Urdu invention, maker and work accounts; distinguishes decimal Jarida from sexagesimal Sidra, inherited arithmetic from a specific application, and modern teaching notation from manuscript evidence. Preserves original maker attribution/occupation and uses a reverified public-domain modern triangle, not an Ottoman artifact.
- Invention54: Ibn Bassal's linked assessment of land, water, season and crops, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu with new maker/book accounts. Actual selected Arabic supports ten practical land types and four water sources, not invention of farming, modern soil chemistry or drip irrigation. Life/textual uncertainties remain explicit; the inspected public-domain modern horizon drawing is comparative context, not his classification. No unsafe handling instructions or deployment.
- Invention53: al-Razi's reported intentional patient-group comparison, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. Comparison, record keeping and uncertainty are distinguished from a modern randomized trial or proof of treatment effectiveness. Approximate numbers, missing group data, source-location discrepancy and disputed identity stay explicit; the reused public-domain title page is different-book context only. Earlier maker contributions and attribution retained; no treatment instructions or deployment.
- Invention52: al-Zarqali's moving solar-apogee and variable-eccentricity model, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. The lost solar work, probable dating, indirect transmission and limits of a modern unit conversion are explicit; the public-domain image explains apsides, not a reconstructed medieval model. Earlier maker contributions and attribution retained; al-Sufi stays an uncounted lead pending qualifying identity evidence. Content only, no deployment.
- Invention43: al-Jazari's musical boat, complete Arabic/English/Urdu accounts and an inspected public-domain1315 manuscript image. Finite water, intermittent movement and whistling remain distinct from modern programming; the non-touching harp fingers, peg-count discrepancy, missing sailor drive and lengthening intervals are explicit. Existing maker contributions and attribution retained. Content only, no deployment.
- Invention42: al-Zahrawi's differentiated dental scrapers, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. Actual chapter29 and edition-specific figure numbers are distinguished; manuscript variation and the editors' originality assessment remain bounded. Earlier maker contributions are preserved. The reused public-domain image is explicitly contextual, not authenticated scrapers; no treatment instructions, modern-tool pedigree or deployment.
- Invention41: al-Karaji's levelling and tunnel-alignment methods, complete Arabic/English/Urdu accounts, inspected late-manuscript diagram and preserved maker attribution. Older qanats remain older; copy dates, modern interpretations and measurement limits stay explicit. Content only, no deployment.
- Invention40: al-Jazari's castle water clock, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. Seasonal hours, empirical flow calibration and the attendant's work remain visible; no first-computer claim. An inspected public-domain1354 manuscript painting accompanies selected primary evidence and the preserved maker biography, with book-date/manuscript differences explicit. Content only, no deployment.
- Invention39: al-Biruni's conical vessel collecting displaced water for relative-density measurement, with complete Arabic, English and Urdu accounts and the preserved, expanded maker biography. The inspected public-domain printed diagram is not surviving hardware; manuscript gaps, drainage errors, disputed affiliation and later al-Khazini trials remain explicit. A modern paper-only example distinguishes dimensionless ratios from density units. Content only, no hazardous procedures, new code or deployment.
- Invention38: al-Razi's nested substance classification and structured alchemical handbook, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. Six earthy-material classes are not a periodic table; uncertain names, edition limits, genuine/spurious works and disputed religious readings remain explicit. Three defined sources, a credited public-domain modern halite comparison and the preserved maker biography accompany the account. No hazardous recipes, deployment or new code.
- Invention37: al-Razi's documented clinical comparison of smallpox and measles, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. The inspected translated primary supports relative comparison, not all diagnosis, virology or vaccination; unknown composition date and conflicting religious readings remain explicit. Seven defined sources and a credited public-domain1848 translation title page accompany the preserved, expanded maker biography. Historical description only, no clinical instructions or deployment.
- Invention36: Ibn al-Haytham's fourth-power/exhaustion volume method, full Arabic/English/Urdu account and preserved maker biography. Perpendicular-axis8/15 distinguished from ordinary axial1/2 and modern reconstruction from historical claims; five defined sources and licensed explicitly comparative image. Content only, no deployment.

- A thirty-fifth invention joins the browser: Abu al-Wafa's organized one-opening compass constructions, with complete Arabic, English and Urdu accounts. The inspected translated primary retains its Persian/pupil transmission layers and radius-choice caveat; checked modern examples distinguish a valid perpendicular from degenerate cases and universal claims. Woepcke's tentative European-transmission hypothesis and Raynaud's bounded independent-discovery analysis remain attributed, without a museum verdict. The credited CC BY-SA3.0 photograph is explicitly a modern adjustable tool, not his compass.
- A thirty-fourth invention joins the browser: Abu al-Wafa's simpler spherical-triangle solution methods, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. The selected translated primary's proof and explicit priority dispute remain together; no first-trigonometry, first-tangent or lunar-variation claim. A checked modern numerical example distinguishes angular sides from planar lengths, and the credited public-domain diagram is explicitly modern. His expanded biography preserves earlier attribution while separating sourced Muslim identity from the declared Sunni frame.
- A thirty-third invention joins the browser: Khayyam's systematic geometric solutions of cubic equations, fully told in Arabic, English and Urdu. Earlier solutions, fourteen positive-coefficient forms and limits on roots and priority remain visible; an inspected translated primary and a credited public-domain modern diagram support a worked example that explicitly excludes the misleading origin. His expanded biography separates the declared Sunni frame, sourced Muslim identity and conflicting interpretations.
- A thirty-second invention joins the browser: al-Zahrawi's account of animal-intestinal suturing filaments, with complete Arabic, English and Urdu articles. The inspected translated primary page explicitly retains its experimental caveat and inherited suturing knowledge; no claim to the first suture or modern clinical safety. A credited public-domain photograph is clearly a modern material comparison, not his thread or a certified sterile product; no operative instructions are reproduced.
- A thirty-first invention joins the browser: the fifth oud string attributed to Ziryab, with complete Arabic, English and Urdu accounts distinguishing an inherited instrument from a reported refinement. Maqqari's actual string order and symbolism sit beside modern criticism of the idealized biography; no universal-first or guaranteed guitar-descent claim. The reused CC0 Cleveland photograph is explicitly a much later instrument, not his oud or a reconstruction.
- A thirtieth invention joins the browser: Ibn Firnas's reported winged flight, told in Arabic, English and Urdu as a disputed account rather than a verified first airplane. Maqqari's injury and Hasan Ibrahim Hasan's unharmed landing remain distinct; uncertain dates, missing measurements and modern retelling caveats are visible. A licensed photograph shows the modern Cordoba bridge bearing his name, explicitly not surviving wings or a reconstruction.
- A twenty-ninth invention joins the browser: al-Battani's sine-table computation, with complete Arabic, English and Urdu accounts grounded in the inspected Arabic chapter and Latin translation. Radius60, half-degree rows, forward and inverse interpolation are distinguished from invention of sine itself. The date is an explicitly open lifetime bound, and the credited public-domain triangle is modern teaching geometry, not a manuscript image.
- A twenty-eighth invention joins the browser: al-Biruni's mountain-and-horizon Earth measurement, with complete Arabic, English and Urdu accounts grounded in the actual translated Nandana experiment. The earlier Sanad ibn Ali account, disputed priority and personal affiliation remain labelled; historical cubits are not turned into spectacular modern accuracy claims. A credited modern geometry illustration is explicitly not an original manuscript.
- A twenty-seventh invention joins the browser: al-Zarqali's independent two-plate equatorium, with Arabic, English and Urdu explanations distinguishing it from his universal astrolabe. Ibn al-Samh's earlier instrument remains visible, construction and use treatises are dated separately, and Mercury's oval is not presented as Kepler's orbital law. The credited photograph is explicitly a much later Liverpool instrument, not an original or reconstruction.
- The Inventions Browser opens at /ar/inventions/, /en/inventions/ and /ur/inventions/ with its first sourced record, Ibn al-Nafis's account of the pulmonary circulation: the domains, an A-to-Z list with a name filter, and an Explore menu entry and landing card. The invention page shows the Wellcome manuscript image with its credit, quick facts, the makers, the claim and its basis, what came before and after, and a diagram drawn at build time.
- A second invention joins the browser: Rufayda's medical tent in the Prophet's Mosque at Medina, where Sa'd ibn Mu'adh was cared for after the Battle of the Trench, told from Ibn Hajar's al-Isaba with the open question of what counts as the first hospital in Islam set out without a verdict, a 1908 photograph of the mosque and a diagram of the path from the Trench to the tent.
- A third invention joins the browser: the first minarets, which Maslama ibn Mukhallad, governor of Egypt, had built at the corners of the Mosque of Amr in Fustat in 53/673, told from Ibn Abd al-Hakam, al-Maqrizi and Ibn Hajar, with a 2011 photograph of the mosque and a diagram of what came before and after.
- A fourth invention joins the browser: the barid, the relay post of stations and fresh mounts that Mu'awiya ibn Abi Sufyan set up for the caliphate on the Persian and Byzantine model and Abd al-Malik perfected, told from al-Qalqashandi's Subh al-A'sha and al-Suyuti's Tarikh al-Khulafa, with a modern map of the Yemeni barid stations and a diagram of what came before and after it, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- A fifth invention joins the browser: the bimaristan that al-Maqrizi says the Umayyad caliph al-Walid ibn Abd al-Malik built in 88/707, the first in Islam on his account, with physicians paid by the state and allowances for lepers and the blind, told from al-Maqrizi's Khitat, Ibn Kathir and al-Suyuti and set beside Rufayda's tent as a contested first without a verdict, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- A sixth invention joins the browser: the tiraz, the band of writing woven into the caliphate's garments, which al-Baladhuri, al-Suyuti and Ibn Khaldun show Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan turning from Persian images and Greek writing to the remembrance of God, the change that led to the first Islamic dinars, with a CC0 tiraz fragment from the Metropolitan Museum, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- A seventh invention joins the browser: algebra, al-jabr wal-muqabala, which al-Khwarizmi set out in Baghdad under al-Ma'mun as a science of three kinds of term and six types of equation, each with a rule and a drawn proof, read in his own book with Ibn Khaldun's account and TDV's survey of the priority question, with a public-domain Bodleian manuscript image, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- An eighth invention joins the browser: the Tabula Rogeriana, the world al-Idrisi drew at Palermo for Roger II, engraved on a silver disc and laid out in his book *Nuzhat al-mushtaq* as seventy maps, each followed by its description, read in the book's own preface with TDV's survey, with Konrad Miller's public-domain copy of the maps, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- A ninth invention joins the browser: the camera obscura, Ibn al-Haytham's dark room in *Kitab al-Manazir*, where light through a small hole is followed through dust, cut by a body and checked with a ruler, and candles before one aperture cast separate spots that do not mix, read in Sabra's edition of the Arabic text with TDV's survey, with a page of al-Farisi's *Tanqih al-Manazir*, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- A tenth invention joins the browser: algorism, al-Khwarizmi's book on Indian reckoning, through which the nine numerals and zero entered the Islamic world and, in its twelfth-century Latin translation opening "Dixit Algorizmi", reached Europe, where his name became *Algorismus* and the word *algorithm*, read in the Cairo edition of *al-Jabr*, Ibn Khaldun and TDV's survey, with the Cambridge manuscript page, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- An eleventh invention joins the browser: the hisba, the market office of Medina whose officers, appointed by the Prophet and by Umar, among them a woman, al-Shifa' bint Abd Allah, tested weights, kept the streets clear and checked the bakers, and whose holder became the muhtasib under the Abbasids and the *almotacén* of Spain.
- A twelfth invention joins the browser: the waqf, the endowment whose formula, "hold back the capital and give the yield in charity", came in the Prophet's answer to Umar about his land at Khaybar, and which went on to fund the mosques, colleges, hospitals, fountains and soup kitchens of Muslim cities for a thousand years.
- A thirteenth invention joins the browser: Harun al-Rashid's Baghdad bimaristan, presented in Arabic, English and Urdu as transmission of the Jundishapur hospital model, not invention of hospitals. The caliph is credited as patron and the physicians by their own names; TDV's explicit identification of the Bukhtishu family as Nestorian Christian also corrects Jibril's unsourced Sunni tag. The photograph is clearly identified as a later Damascus hospital, not the lost Baghdad building.
- A fourteenth invention joins the browser: Ibn Tulun's endowed civilian hospital, whose account describes safekeeping of patients' belongings, supplied clothing and bedding, food, medicine and discharge. Arabic, English and Urdu articles preserve the conflicting dates and admission exclusions without claiming the first hospital; a contextual photograph shows his surviving mosque, not the lost institution.
- A fifteenth invention joins the browser: Nizam al-Mulk's endowed madrasa network, with teachers, books, accommodation and student support, in Arabic, English and Urdu. The account distinguishes earlier Nishapur from the famous Baghdad foundation and does not claim the first school, university or degree. The reused Baghdad building summary also distinguishes its first teachers from al-Juwayni at Nishapur; the photograph is explicitly the later Mustansiriyya.
- A sixteenth invention joins the browser: al-Jazari's elephant clock, with the Met manuscript folio and its documented half-hour sequence of moving figures, a ball and a gong. Arabic, English and Urdu pages distinguish the engineer from the later copyist, and the original design from the surviving 1315 drawing, without a “first robot” claim.
- A seventeenth invention joins the browser: al-Jazari's waterwheel-driven double-piston pump, with its gear-and-slotted-rod drive explained in Arabic, English and Urdu. The historical drawing and half-size museum reconstruction are distinguished, the book's 1205/1206 date difference remains visible, and claims about suction-pipe priority and later engines are attributed rather than turned into an unsupported direct lineage.
- An eighteenth record joins the browser: al-Jazari's peacock hand-washing design, with its water-and-float soap/towel sequence in Arabic, English and Urdu. Cleveland's warning that actual construction is uncertain stays explicit; the surviving 1315 folio and CC0 image are reused and their book label corrected without changing legacy IDs.
- A nineteenth invention joins the browser: al-Kindi's letter-frequency cryptanalysis, read in the Arabic treatise, with counting, short-message limits and linguistic cross-checks explained in Arabic, English and Urdu. The manuscript opening, critical edition and earliest-surviving-text claim are distinguished; the maker's disputed theological classification is shown through labelled readings, not a museum verdict.
- A twentieth record joins the browser: al-Fadl ibn Yahya's proposal to establish papermaking in Baghdad, presented as transmission of an older Chinese craft, not invention of paper. Arabic, English and Urdu explain fiber preparation and the limits of the workshop evidence, preserve disagreement about the Talas-prisoner story, and clearly label the licensed photograph as modern Konigil craft context.
- A twenty-first record joins the browser: al-Zarqali's universal astrolabe plate, explained in Arabic, English and Urdu as a latitude-independent improvement, not invention of all astrolabes. Coordinate grids, the horizon ruler and different textual variants are distinguished; the licensed Calahorra display-copy photograph is not mistaken for an eleventh-century original or Louvre's later catalogue object.
- A twenty-second record joins the browser: al-Zahrawi's described drill for an obstructing urethral stone, distinguished from bladder-stone forceps in Arabic, English and Urdu. The account explains historical mechanics without surgical instructions or a blanket “first lithotripsy” verdict; a new sourced trilingual biography preserves the limits of early testimony, and the public-domain manuscript image is clearly contextual rather than a confirmed drill picture.
- A twenty-third record joins the browser: al-Kindi's mathematical interpretation of compound-drug degrees, explained in Arabic, English and Urdu as an improvement of an inherited Galenic model, not a modern prescription or proof of clinical efficacy. Latin reception is distinguished from universal practical adoption, the maker's affiliation disagreement remains visible, and the CC0 medicinal jar is explicitly later context rather than his equipment.
- A twenty-fourth record joins the browser: al-Khazini's refined balance of wisdom, explained in Arabic, English and Urdu with earlier contributors, calibrated movable pans, air/liquid weighing and binary-alloy limits. The sensitivity remains a historical claim rather than a modern replication; Penn's public-domain manuscript diagram is a later witness, not original hardware, and the maker's own preface supplies the cited Muslim basis.
- A twenty-fifth record joins the browser: al-Karaji's arithmetic of powers and composite expressions, in Arabic, English and Urdu. It distinguishes this improvement from all algebra, general polynomial division, rigorous modern induction and modern symbols; historians' different assessments and retained geometrical proofs stay visible. A credited CC BY-SA3.0 teaching image is explicitly modern, and the maker gains a sourced trilingual biography.
- A twenty-sixth record joins the browser: al-Uqlidisi's decimal-fraction operations in his Damascus341/952–953 book, with the units-place sign and historians' differing assessments explained in Arabic, English and Urdu. The record distinguishes composition from the later manuscript copy, modern notation from the historical mark, and inspected source text from missing formulae. A CC0 modern chart and enriched maker biography accompany it.
- Guidance for every agent on borrow-only books: they are borrowed and read in the browser one request at a time, the founder signs in when asked, and pages are OCR'd from screenshots outside git.
- The machine-readable foundation for the Muslim Inventions Browser: a new invention record class and sourced links to related people, places, works, objects and concepts.
- Static API exports for invention records and their precursor and dependent chains.
- The research queue now contains 515 leads (476 active and 39 cited exclusions), including three page-anchored claims from the *1001 Inventions* Home chapter; an early-engineering screen leaves al-Muradi's gearing, the saqiya improvement and Sistan windmill claims unresolved, while concept screens excluded twelve non-invention signals, generic Ibn Taymiyya, Kamal Khujandi and Ulubatlı Hasan census signals and the Alpujarras Rebellion event hit were closed as unrelated, and the generic chess-transmission, kohl, miswak and turban leads were closed with cited reasons. Twenty-six invention/design records have been published in the dataset; the peacock device's physical construction remains uncertain, and a specifically post-conversion al-Samawal calculation remains held for unverified chronology. Wider binomial-triangle and al-Kashi leads remain open without double-counting the specifically covered methods.
- A registered 1908 Arabic scan now supports the Fakhri sextant's location at Rayy and preserves al-Biruni's account of the instrument; al-Khujandi's Sunni basis and the exact scale remain unresolved, so the lead has not been promoted.
- An early-institutions screen adds source links for minarets, tiraz, barid, hisba and waqf, and keeps three distinct bimaristan claims as provisional leads rather than a settled “first hospital” chronology; the registered TDV entry calls Rufayda's tent the first Islamic bimaristan, but its source chain and the meaning of “first” remain unresolved.
- Relevant sections of five independent scholarly references have been read and their scope and limitations recorded before invention writing.
- Seven contemporary figures join the People wing, opening the museum's Ethiopian coverage: the menzuma artist Muaz Habib, the Afaan Oromo teacher Anwar Yusuf and the comparative-religion preacher Wahid Omar Negash from Ethiopia; the Bangladeshi scholars Ahmadullah, Abu Taw Haa Muhammad Adnan and Enayetullah Abbasi; and the Senegalese preacher Abdoul Wahab Dièye — each with sourced pages in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Five Islamic media groups join the collection: Minber TV and Nejashi TV from Ethiopia, Africa TV from Nigeria, and Taw Haa Tv and Holy Tune from Bangladesh — channels carrying sermons, lectures and nasheed to audiences from tens of thousands to over ten million subscribers, each with sourced pages in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- An index of the embeddable widgets at /ar/embed/, /en/embed/ and /ur/embed/, listing "Did you know?" and "Trail of the week" with the code to place each on another site.
- Eight contemporary Urdu-language scholars and preachers from Pakistan and India join the People wing: Faiz Syed of IRC TV (4.17 million YouTube subscribers); Dawat-e-Islami's founder Ilyas Qadri and the preacher Abdul Habib Attari; the late Khadim Hussain Rizvi, founder of Tehreek-e-Labbaik Pakistan, whose archive channel still publishes his recordings; Mufti Salman Azhari; Ashraf Asif Jalali; and Minhaj-ul-Quran's Hussain and Hassan Mohi-ud-Din Qadri — each with sourced pages in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Seven more contemporary Urdu-language scholars and preachers from Pakistan and India join the People wing: Youth Club's Tuaha Ibn Jalil; the naat reciter Owais Raza Qadri; Dawat-e-Islami's council head Imran Attari and Ubaid Raza Attari; the debater Ilyas Ghumman; the Qur'an teacher Nighat Hashmi; and the speaker Sahil Adeem — each with sourced pages in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Four Islamic media and da'wah organisations from Pakistan join the collection: Madani Channel, the television station of Dawat-e-Islami (3.63 million YouTube subscribers); Youth Club (1.71 million); Dawat-e-Islami's own channel (1.15 million); and Minhaj TV of Minhaj-ul-Quran International (146,000) — each with sourced pages in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- The coverage-expansion programme gains its first phase: the AI-resource moves — making the Companions a queryable set, completing the Prophet's structured neighbourhood, reconciling event rosters, marking hadith narrators, and extending the pages' machine-readable markup — folded into the programme plan on the founder's go, with the audit behind it re-measured against the current graph (474 Companion records; the Prophet's record still carries 6 relations). The programme's later phases are renumbered 2–15 to make room.
- A pull request that touches a file baked into every page (a layout, the header or footer, a shell string in `i18n/`) is now labelled `rewrites-all-pages` and its summary says what the next deploy will cost, so such changes can be batched into one deploy. The list of those files is `tools/deploy/shell-paths.list`.

### Changed

- The open-data guide now documents all 23 schema classes and the invention API endpoints.
- Entry pages no longer show a "Draft" label or a "Completeness n/5" score, which are internal workflow notes; the invitation to help complete an entry stays, next to "Report an error".
- The overview map in the Places wing is easier to read: names no longer pile on top of each other. The best-connected places are labelled first and a name that would cover another is left off; every place's dot is now a link with its name, so every place on the map can still be opened.
- CONTRIBUTING.md rewritten for outside contributors: it now describes how the museum actually works — no human review gate, one cited source is enough, all three languages in the same commit — and documents setup, the validation gates, the 25-entity PR limit and the canary rule itself, replacing a short guide that still promised wing-editor approvals and draft-until-reviewed AI content.
- An engineering proposal explains why every daily deploy re-uploaded 174,000 to 206,000 files to R2 although the publisher only sends changed bytes: the stylesheet, the layout scripts and the version line sit inside every one of the 164,000 pages, so any edit to them rewrites the whole site. It measures the cost, surveys what other static hosts and build tools do, and proposes stable pages (external stylesheet and scripts, version filled from the deploy stamp, deterministic EPUBs, a churn report in the deploy summary, a label for changes that rewrite all pages) before any change to how pages are served. Nothing is implemented yet; the founder decides (`docs/engineering/16-deploy-churn-proposal.md`).
- The menu, theme and accessibility script and the entity page's small scripts are now plain files (`/shell/shell.js`, `/shell/entity.js`) instead of being copied into each of the 164,000 pages, and the footer fills in the release number after the page loads from the deploy's stamp file. The pages look and behave the same, and without JavaScript the footer shows the changelog link without the number. The reason is cost: a change to those scripts or a new release meant uploading every page again on the next deploy (`docs/engineering/16-deploy-churn-proposal.md`). The site stylesheet stays inside the pages: linking it was measured to slow the first paint by about 300 ms on a slow phone and to fail the performance check on the Arabic and Urdu home pages, so it waits for a better way (same document).
- The accessible e-books (EPUB) now record the day their source last changed instead of the minute they were built, so a book changes only when its content does.

### Fixed

- The static-site guard no longer fails on the search page's synonym files: it read the start of the address the page builds for each synonym piece (`/search-synonyms.` + language + shard + `.json`) and mistook it for a non-static path, which stopped the build. It now judges a built address by its last piece, so the whole address must still end in a static file; calls such as `/ask/` + query are still rejected, and tests cover both.
- The Connections Explorer loads again: the site's content security policy now allows pages to fetch from the museum's API host (api.islam.museum), where the explorer's graph-data requests are redirected — the policy previously blocked those loads, so every entry failed with a "could not load" message.
- The museum's API catalog at /.well-known/api-catalog, the machine-readable list of its data services that every page's Link header points to, is published again. The build kept the site in an archive that silently leaves out folders whose names begin with a dot, so the catalog never reached the live site and the address answered "not found"; the archive now keeps them, and the catalog is served with the other root data files.
- The Hijri calendar's "Today is" line, highlighted day and opening month now follow the reader's own date, worked out in the browser with the same conversion as the Time Machine. They had been fixed on the day the site was built. Without JavaScript the page still shows a complete month.
- A page that does not exist under /en/ or /ur/ now says so in English or Urdu instead of Arabic; the rest of the site keeps the Arabic page.
- Each exhibit now appears once on the exhibits list in every language. The two Alexandria trails were one trail recorded twice: they are now one, and the old address still leads to it. "Great Mosques of the Garrison Cities" and «دول المغرب الأدنى» (States of the Maghreb) are different trails that shared a title with another, and now have their own.
- The Urdu embed index (/ur/embed/) now reads in Urdu; it had been added with English placeholder text.
- On the Places wing overview map, no dot now covers a place name and no two names touch: names are drawn above the dots, and the space each name needs is measured as it actually renders, including Arabic script.
- Every build of the site stopped on 6 November because one record (Ibn Mu'adh al-Jayyani, whose death is written "1079 or later") has a date with no end, and the timeline step could not handle it. A date with an open end is now read as the one point that is known, so the build runs again and no date is invented. A date open on both sides is still rejected.

### Tests

- A connections diagram for every connected record: the tests and the API export now fail if any record with at least one typed connection to another record has no Connections Explorer file, if a diagram is not centred on its record or draws a link that is not a real connection, or if an entry page offers "explore connections" without a diagram behind it. Records linked only through shared themes have no diagram by design; their "see also" list is a recommendation, not a connection.
- Tests for the deterministic e-book stamp, the pull-request deploy-impact check, and the new churn report in the R2 publisher.
- The performance check on pull requests (Lighthouse) now asks for a score of at least 0.90 instead of 0.95; the accessibility check still requires a perfect 1.0. The old minimum failed pull requests that changed nothing visible, because the Arabic and Urdu home pages scored exactly 0.95 on a good run and 0.93 to 0.94 whenever the build servers were busy. Founder's decision, 6 November 2026.
- The test run no longer fails after every test has passed. Long tests that check the whole site left no turn for the test runner's own messages, so it reported "Timeout calling onTaskUpdate" and the Build workflow cancelled the build; this stopped two of three pull request checks and a build of main on 6 November. Every test file now gives the runner a turn before each test; no test is skipped.

### Performance

- Search starts faster, above all on phones and slow connections: the list of variant spellings (Saladin, Salah al-Din…) is now split into small pieces and the search page fetches only the few it needs for the words typed, about 14 KB each, instead of the whole list on every visit (2 MB compressed, 10 MB in Arabic and Urdu). Each search still waits for its pieces, so results and counts are the same as before.
- The repository checks and the test suite repeat less work: the prose files, the compiled schemas and the frozen Tanzil files are now read once per run and reused, where each validation call read them again. Nothing the checks decide has changed, and a Tanzil file that is edited is still read again and still fails its recorded hash. This matters because the full test suite ran for about 14 minutes on the shared build machines and one run failed on a test-runner timeout, not on a rule.
- The tests that check one made-up record no longer re-check the whole repository: `validateAll` has a fixture mode (`{ onlyExtra: true }`) that applies every rule to the supplied records only, using the repository just as context (ids, aliases, stubs, sources, external ids, near-duplicate titles). Each such test drops from tens of seconds to well under a second, and the one test that validates everything still does. The rules are unchanged and no test or timeout was relaxed; this removes the load that made the test runner time out.
- Deploys upload less: changing the page scripts or releasing a new version now changes one file instead of all 164,000 pages, and the e-books no longer change on every build. Each deploy's summary has a new "R2 publish churn" table that shows what was uploaded by group, estimates the cost, and warns, naming the shell files that changed, when more than a fifth of the pages are re-uploaded. `tools/deploy/compare-builds.py` compares two builds file by file.

### Data
- Passages 4:127 and 4:128 (volume 9, pp. 257-268, reports 10550-10574): the orphan girls of 4:127 and the second to fourth views on what is recited, al-Tabari's choice of the inheritance verses, the two readings of the desire to marry or avoid marrying them, the weak children and standing for orphans in fairness (reports from Qatada, al-Suddi, Aisha, Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, Umar and Ali), and the first clause of 4:128 on a wife who fears ill-treatment, with the editor Shakir's notes. Given as transmitted, with no museum verdict. The stop sentence at p. 256 was removed; 4:128 carries a stop sentence at p. 268 before report 10575.
- Passages 4:123 to 4:127 (volume 9, pp. 246-256, reports 10533-10549): the last reports on misfortunes as requital and the closing clause on ally and helper (4:123); 4:124 on who enters Paradise, the grain-groove and the word 'of'; 4:125 on the best religion and Abraham as friend, with the account of the sand turned to flour; 4:126 on God's ownership and encompassing knowledge; and the first clause of 4:127 (the ruling on women and orphan girls, reports 10539-10549, with the editor Shakir's notes). Given as transmitted, with no museum verdict. The stop sentence at p. 246 was removed; 4:127 is cut after report 10549 on p. 256 and carries a stop sentence.
- Passage 4:123, second clause (volume 9, pp. 235-246, reports 10507-10532): the three views on the word for evil (every act of disobedience, per Ubayy ibn Ka'b and Aisha; the disbelievers; shirk), al-Tabari's choice of the first, his answer on how requital fits the promise of expiation, and the reports from the Prophet that misfortunes in this world are that requital (10520-10532), as transmitted, with the editor Shakir's chain notes and gradings attributed to him. No museum verdict. The stop sentence at p. 235 was removed; the file is cut after report 10532 on p. 246 and carries a stop sentence.
- Passages 4:121 to 4:123 (volume 9, pp. 225-235, reports 10490-10506): 4:121 on the abode of Jahannam and the word for escape; 4:122 on God's true promise against the devil's; 4:123, opening clause, on whom it addresses (the Muslims, 10490-10499; the idol-worshipping polytheists, 10500-10505; the People of the Book alone, 10506), with al-Tabari's choice of Mujahid's view and the editor Shakir's notes. The library note for this source also gains the volume 8 scan-oddities log for pp. 440-446.
- Passage 4:46 (volume 8, pp. 433-439, reports 9693-9712): the statement of hearing and going against the order (9693-9696, Mujahid, Ibn Zayd), the phrase of address that denies hearing and the readings of Mujahid and al-Hasan with al-Tabari's reply (9697-9702), the word of two meanings twisted by the tongue (9703-9707), what the Jews would have said had they spoken well and al-Tabari's discussion of Mujahid's and Ikrima's glosses (9708-9711), the editor's long note on the verses of al-Hutay'a and Ubaydallah b. Qays al-Ruqayyat quoted there (described, not reproduced), and the close of the verse on the driving away of the Jews and their little belief (9712), with cross-links to 2:104, 2:88 and 73:6; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict. The library note for this source also gains the volume 8 scan-oddities log for pp. 433-439.
- Passages 4:43, 4:44, 4:45 and 4:46 (volume 8, pp. 423-432, reports 9673-9692): in 4:43 the report of Ibrahim on the person in janaba (9673), al-Tabari's choice that such a person is among those commanded to do tayammum (his account and arguments), whether tayammum is renewed for every prayer (the view of Ali, Ibn Umar, al-Sha'bi, Qatada and al-Nakha'i, reports 9674-9680, and the view of al-Hasan and Ata that one tayammum serves while no hadath occurs, reports 9681-9686), al-Tabari's choice, and the close of the verse on pardon and covering; in 4:44 the sense of the seeing formula, who is meant (Rifa'a b. Zayd, reports 9687-9690), the sense of choosing misguidance and the wish to mislead the believers; in 4:45 God's knowledge of the hostility and the call to trust Him; in 4:46 the two constructions of the clause on the Jews (Kufan and Basran grammarians), al-Tabari's choice and the sense of altering the words (reports 9691-9692), with the editor's notes on manuscript and print readings; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict. The library note for this source also gains the volume 8 scan-oddities log for pp. 391-432.
- Passage 4:43 (volume 8, pp. 415-422, reports 9664-9672): the rest of the view that the wiping of the hands reaches the elbows (9664-9667, al-Sha'bi, Salim, al-Hasan) with its argument and the report of Abu Juhaym (9668) and the editor's notes on its chain and on the sounder version, the view that the limit is the armpits (9669-9670, al-Zuhri, Ammar) with the editor's notes on the connected routes, al-Tabari's choice that the palms to the wrists are the minimum and beyond that the person is free (his), the question whether a person in janaba is included in the concession of tayammum with both groups' arguments (his account), the exchange of Abdullah b. Mas'ud and Abu Musa (9671) and of Umar and Ammar (9672), with a cross-link to 5:6; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:43 (volume 8, pp. 408-415, reports 9644-9663): the senses of the word for the earth (9644-9646) with al-Tabari's choice and his gloss of a line of Dhu al-Rumma (described), the sense of the word for good (9647-9648), the start of the clause on wiping the face and hands with al-Tabari's account of the manner and the dust (his), the view that the limit is the palms to the wrists (9649-9657, Ammar, al-Sha'bi, Ikrima, Makhul, the reports of Ammar and Umar) with the editor's notes on chains and printed readings, and the view that the wiping reaches the elbows (9658-9663, Ibn Umar, al-Hasan, al-Sha'bi), with cross-links to 5:6 and 5:38; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:43 (volume 8, pp. 400-407, reports 9634-9643): the reports on the occasion of the verse, the Companions with wounds in janaba (9634) and the lack of water on a journey, with the necklace story of Aisha (9635-9636, 9639-9642, from Aisha, Ibn Abbas, Ibn Abi Mulayka, Urwa) and the story of al-Asla' (9637-9638), with the editor's notes on chains, gradings and misreadings of the printed text; al-Tabari's remark that the two readings of the clause on touching are close in meaning (his); and the start of the next clause with his gloss of the verb of intending (9643, Sufyan); given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:43 (volume 8, pp. 391-399, reports 9590-9633): the remaining reports that the touching of women means intercourse (9590-9605, Ibn Abbas, Ali, al-Hasan, Mujahid, Qatada) with the Arab and mawali dispute at Ibn Abbas's door, the view that every touch is meant and ablution required (9606-9628, Ibn Mas'ud, Ibn Umar, Ubayda's gesture, al-Sha'bi, Ibrahim, al-Hakam, Hammad, Ata') with the editor's notes on names and readings, al-Tabari's choice of intercourse on the basis of the reports of the kiss without fresh ablution (9629-9632) with the editor's notes on the chains and on the Urwa identity, and the report from Umm Salama (9633) with al-Tabari's argument and his gloss of a word in a cited line of verse (his; the editor calls it unusual); given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:43 (volume 8, pp. 383-390, reports 9555-9589): the rest of the view that passing through the mosque is meant (9555-9569) with the editor's note on a gap in a chain, al-Tabari's choice of that view and his reasoning from the later clause, his note on the word for the passer (his), the sick with wounds, sores, smallpox or fractures (9570-9579) with the editor's notes on names and on the phrase about the horsemen, al-Tabari's sense of the remaining clauses and of the low ground (9580), and the reports on the touching of women meaning intercourse according to Ibn Abbas against the view of less than intercourse (9581-9589); given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passages 4:42 and 4:43 (volume 8, pp. 375-382, reports 9524-9554): for 4:42 (closed), the rest of al-Tabari's statement of the sense on the view from Ibn Abbas and the other view, with the editor's textual notes; for 4:43 (begun), al-Tabari's gloss of the opening, the reports that the intoxication meant is from drink (9524-9532) with the occasion reports and the editor's notes, the view that it is from sleep (9533-9534), al-Tabari's choice and his answer to the objection about the drunk and the mad (his), the reports that the person in major impurity is the traveller who finds no water (9535-9551) with the editor's notes on the chains, and the view that passing through the mosque is meant (9552-9554); given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passages 4:40, 4:41 and 4:42 (volume 8, pp. 367-374, reports 9511-9523): for 4:40 (closed), report 9511 on the emigrants and the desert dwellers with the editor's tracings, al-Tabari's choice reconciling the reports on multiplying (his), and reports 9512-9514 on the recompense; for 4:41 (filed in full), the sense of the verse, the prophets' and Muhammad's community's witness (9515-9517), and reports 9518-9519 on the Prophet's words and Ibn Mas'ud's recitation with the editor's long note on their chains; for 4:42 (begun), the sense, the three readings of the verb for levelling with al-Tabari's preference (his), reports 9520-9523 from Ibn Abbas on concealing and not concealing, up to the start of al-Tabari's statement of the sense on that view; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passages 4:39 and 4:40 (volume 8, pp. 359-366, reports 9502-9510): for 4:39 (closed), al-Tabari's sense of the verse; for 4:40 (begun), God not diminishing those who spend, the sense of the small unit of weight (9502-9504), the reports on rewarding the believer and the long report on intercession (9505-9507) with the editor's tracings, Ibn Mas'ud's account of settling wrongs on the day of resurrection (9508-9509) with the editor's gradings, al-Tabari's reading and choice, the two readings of the word for a good deed, the form of the verb for multiplying, and report 9510 on how many times a good deed is multiplied, up to the view that the promise is for the emigrants only; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passages 4:36, 4:37 and 4:38 (volume 8, pp. 350-358, reports 9491-9501): for 4:36 (closed), the sense of the boastful and reports 9491-9492; for 4:37, the grammar of the relative, the meaning of stinginess and the two readings (9493), the view that the verse concerns the Jews who concealed Muhammad's description (9494-9501, with the editor's notes on the tribes named in 9501), al-Tabari's argument and choice, and the close of the verse; for 4:38, the sense of spending for display and who is meant, the separating conjunction, and the gloss on the close friend with a line of Adi b. Zayd described not reproduced; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:36 (volume 8, pp. 340-349, reports 9457-9490, then the opening of the gloss on the last clause): the end of al-Tabari's account of the sense of distance and the editor's note on a line of al-A'sha; the three views of the companion at one's side (fellow-traveller 9457-9470, wife 9471-9479, one attached hoping for benefit 9480-9481) with al-Tabari's choice that all are meant and the two reports 9482-9483 with the editor's gradings; the passing traveller and the guest (9484-9489) with al-Tabari's choice; those owned and the right-hand phrasing (9490); and the start of the gloss on the proud one with two lines of poetry described not reproduced; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings the editor's, and no museum verdict. Also adds the volume 8 oddities from p. 292 on to the library note.
- Passages 4:35 and 4:36 (volume 8, pp. 332-339, reports 9430-9456): for 4:35, the reports 9430-9436 that the clause on intending reconciliation concerns the two judges, and al-Tabari's gloss on the close of the verse; for 4:36 (begun), al-Tabari's gloss on worship without associates and on kindness to parents, relatives, orphans and the poor, then the interpreters' views on the neighbour who is a relative (reports 9437-9445, with al-Tabari's reply to Maymun b. Mihran) and on nearness by Islam (9446), and on the neighbour described as distant (9447-9456), with al-Tabari's preference and a line of al-A'sha described not reproduced; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:35 (volume 8, pp. 324-331, reports 9414-9429): the end of the editor's note on report 9413, the reports 9414-9417 on the two judges and the husband's release, the reports 9418-9427 on the view that the judges' ruling stands for joining and separating (with the editor's source tracings and a note on a dating point in Sa'id b. Jubayr's life), report 9428, al-Tabari's choice on who is addressed to send the judges, report 9429, and his rule on what the judges may and may not decide; given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passages 4:34 and 4:35 (volume 8, pp. 316-323, reports 9395-9413): for 4:34, report 9395 and the interpreters' reports 9396-9402 on the closing clause about obedience (what husbands are told about obedient wives, the editor's notes on a word and a verse of poetry described not reproduced), and al-Tabari's gloss on the close of the verse; for 4:35, al-Tabari's account of fear as knowing and of the breach, report 9403, the differences over who is addressed to send the two judges (ruler or spouses, reports 9404-9406), over what they are sent for (as agents of the spouses, with the report of Ali and the editor's source tracings, reports 9407-9410) and the view that they are sent to reconcile and to witness, not to separate (reports 9411-9413); given as transmitted, the choices al-Tabari's, the notes the editor's, and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:34 (volume 8, pp. 308-315, reports 9372-9394): the end of al-Tabari's argument on the word for forsaking and his choice (binding), the reports 9372-9374 on a wife's rights, the reports 9375-9377 and al-Tabari's inference from them, the editor's reply to al-Tabari's choice quoting Ibn al-Arabi, and the interpreters' reports on the kind of striking (9378-9394); the editor's notes and gradings are his, al-Tabari's choices are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:34 continues from p. 316.
- Passage 4:34 (volume 8, pp. 300-307, reports 9335-9371): the rest of the dispute over the meaning of rebellion (9335-9338), the meaning of exhorting (9339-9346, with the editor's notes on the wording of 9342), the four views on forsaking in the bed (leaving intercourse 9347-9352, leaving speech 9353-9357, leaving the bedding 9358-9366 with the editor's note on a narrator, harshness in speech 9367-9371), and the start of al-Tabari's argument on the three senses of the word for forsaking (cut off mid-sentence at the foot of p. 307, with the editor's notes on the two cited lines of verse, described only); the editor's notes and gradings are his, al-Tabari's choices are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:34 continues from p. 308.
- Passage 4:34 (volume 8, pp. 292-299, reports 9307-9335 begun): the occasion reports 9307-9310 and the editor's notes, the meaning of what men spend (9311-9313), the righteous and the devout women (9314-9322), what guarding the unseen means (9323-9328, with the editor's tracing of the report from Abu Hurayra), the dispute over the reading of the divine name in the last clause of that part with al-Tabari's choice (9329-9334, with the editor's note on the reader Abu Jafar), and the start of the dispute over the meaning of fear in the next clause, and of rebellion, to the opening of report 9335 (cut off at the foot of p. 299); the editor's notes and gradings are his, al-Tabari's choices are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:34 continues from p. 300.
- Passages 4:33 and 4:34 (volume 8, pp. 284-291, reports 9293 end-9306): for 4:33, the end of the editor's note on report 9293, report 9294 with the editor's two difficulties in its chains, reports 9295-9299 on the saying that there is no alliance in Islam with the editor's notes on the narrators and sources, al-Tabari's choice (the verse is firm, not abrogated) with the editor's reply to Ibn Kathir's objection, and the close of the verse; for 4:34, the meaning of men standing over their wives (p. 290), reports 9300-9303 and the occasion reports 9304-9306; the editor's notes and gradings are his, al-Tabari's choices are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:34 continues from p. 292.
- Library note `docs/delegation/library/tafsir-tabari-shakir.md`: scan oddities for volume 8 pp. 260-291 recorded (garbled footnotes and verse quotations, the other edition's margin marks, the misprinted p. 280 header, recurring wrong names the editor corrects, footnotes cut at page ends).
- Passage 4:33 continued (volume 8, pp. 276-283, reports 9270 end-9293): the end of report 9270 and reports 9271-9274 on the pre-Islamic alliance and its abrogation, the second view (the pairing in brotherhood, 9275-9276), the third view (help and counsel without inheritance, 9277-9287), the fourth view (adoption, 9288), al-Tabari's choice (the alliance, with the ruling that allies give each other help, counsel and opinion, not inheritance) with reports 9289-9293 on the saying that there is no alliance in Islam, and the editor's notes on narrators, wording and sources (the note on report 9293 cut off at the foot of p. 283); the editor's notes and gradings are his, al-Tabari's choices are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:33 continues from p. 284.
- Passages 4:32 and 4:33 (volume 8, pp. 268-275, reports 9253-9270 begun): the end of 4:32 (the meaning of asking for God's gift, reports 9253-9257 with the editor's notes on the narrators, and the close of the verse); for 4:33, the meaning of the word for heirs (reports 9258-9265, with the cited lines of verse described only), the dispute over the two readings of the binding word with al-Tabari's choice and the editor's notes on the wording, the allies' portion and its abrogation (reports 9266-9269, with the editor's explanation of the pre-Islamic alliance formula), and the start of report 9270 (cut off at the foot of p. 275); the editor's notes and gradings are his, al-Tabari's choices are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:33 continues from p. 276.
- Passages 4:31 and 4:32 (volume 8, pp. 260-267, reports 9235 end-9252): the end of report 9235 on the generous entering in 4:31 and the editor's remarks on the opening of 4:32; for 4:32, the reports on the women's longing (Umm Salama, reports 9236-9244) with the editor's notes on the chains, the grammar of the wording and the connectedness of the report from Mujahid, report 9245, the view that none should long for ranks of excellence (9246-9248), the dispute over what men's and women's portions are (reward and punishment, 9249-9250; inheritance, 9251-9252), and the start of al-Tabari's choice (p. 267, cut mid-commentary); the editor's notes and gradings are his, al-Tabari's choices are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:32 continues from p. 268.

- Library note for al-Tabari (Shakir ed.): corrected the volume 8 oddities entry for pp. 250-259 (misprinted headers are on pp. 251-258, and the p. 259 header misreads as 359).
- Passage 4:31 continued (volume 8, pp. 250-259, reports 9224 end-9235 begun): the end of report 9224 and the editor's note on its narrators, reports 9225-9228 on the major sins (Abu Ayyub, Abu Umama and Ibn Mas'ud) with the editor's gradings and source tracings, al-Tabari's choice reconciling the reports (what the Prophet's reports establish), the meaning of expiation, reports 9229-9234 (al-Suddi, Umar and Abd Allah b. Amr, Anas, Qatada, Ibn Mas'ud, Ibn Abbas), the readers' dispute over the vowelling of the word for the entering with al-Tabari's choice of the damma reading, and the start of the meaning of the generous entering (report 9235, cut off at the foot of p. 259); the editor's notes and gradings are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:31 continues from p. 260.
- Passage 4:31 continued (volume 8, pp. 242-249, reports 9189 note-9224): the end of the editor's note on report 9189, the fourth view that the major sins are four (9190-9200 from Ibn Mas'ud), the fifth that whatever God forbade is a major sin and the reports from Ibn Abbas on a larger number (9201-9210), the sixth view of three (9211), the seventh that every necessitating sin or act threatened with the Fire is major (9212-9218), and the start of al-Tabari's choice resting on reports from the Prophet (9219-9224, cut off at the foot of p. 249); the editor's grading and tracing notes are his, the views are given as transmitted and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:31 continues from p. 250; the library note for al-Tabari (Shakir ed.) records the oddities of volume 8 pp. 234-249.
- Passage 4:31 continued (volume 8, pp. 234-241, reports 9172-9189): the close of the first view on the major sins (reports 9172-9178, with the editor's tracing to al-Haythami), the second view that they are seven (9179 from Ali, 9180-9181 from Ubayd b. Umayr with the Qur'an passages cited by link, 9182-9184 from Ubayda, the ground of that view in report 9185 with the editor's grading and word notes, 9186 from Ata), and the third view that they are nine (9187-9189 from Ibn Umar, with the editor's long note on the reading of one word, his notes on the Najadat and on the transmitters Taysala, Ayyub b. Utba and Sulayman b. Thabit); the wording is al-Tabari's reports as transmitted, the editor's notes are his, and the museum gives no verdict; the commentary on 4:31 continues from p. 242.
- Library note for al-Tabari (Shakir ed.): oddities of volume 8 from p. 186 to p. 233 recorded (smudged and garbled report and verse numbers read by sequence or content, editor's corruption flags, the colophon of the sixth part of the editor's manuscript, and the cut at the foot of p. 233), so later agents and reviewers can see where the printed text needed inference.
- Passages 4:29 closed, 4:30 filed and 4:31 begun (volume 8, pp. 226-233, reports 9163-9172): in 4:29 report 9163 and the first view on consent in trade, the second view that the sale is complete by the contract (as held by Malik, Abu Hanifa, Abu Yusuf and Muhammad per al-Tabari), his choice with his argument and report 9164 and the editor's notes, and the clause on not killing selves with the clause on mercy (9165-9166); in 4:30 the views on what the threat points to (9167), al-Tabari's choice with the editor's praise of its method, the meanings of the words, and the editor's note on the end of the sixth part of his manuscript with its colophon; in 4:31 the first view on the major sins as what is forbidden from the start of the sura to verse thirty (9168-9172, 9172 cut at the foot of p. 233); cross-links to 4:18 and 4:19; cited as {cite:tafsir-tabari-shakir-v08 p. 233}, the next stop is p. 234 in 4:31. Verses touched: 4:29, 4:30, 4:31, 4:18, 4:19. Statements are given as transmitted, with al-Tabari's choices and the editor's notes attributed to them and no museum verdict.
- Passage 4:29 continued (volume 8, pp. 218-225, reports 9143-9162): the report on reluctance to eat at others' houses and its abrogation by a verse of sura 24 (9143), al-Tabari's choice that there is no abrogation because hospitality was never forbidden, the two readings of the word for trade and his preference, his remark on the Sufis who reject earning by trade, the report on the honest trader (9144) with the editor's note on the sound report of the seven in the shade, the meaning of mutual consent in trade (9145-9148), the first view that the option lasts until the two parties separate with the reports from Shurayh, al-Sha'bi, Ibn Sirin and others and the editor's notes on narrators and printed-text errors (9149-9159), and its ground in the reports from Ibn Umar and others (9160-9162); cross-links to 24:61; cited as {cite:tafsir-tabari-shakir-v08 p. 225}, the next stop is p. 226 in 4:29. Verses touched: 4:29, 24:61. Statements are given as transmitted, with al-Tabari's choices and the editor's notes attributed to them and no museum verdict.
- Passages 4:26 closed, 4:27 and 4:28 filed and 4:29 begun (volume 8, pp. 210-217, reports 9129-9142): in 4:26 the second grammatical view that the particles of purpose and 'an' take each other's place after verbs of willing and commanding, with the poetic evidence as described by the editor, the conditions on the exchange and al-Tabari's choice; in 4:27 the meaning of repentance, the views on who follows desires (fornicators, Jews and Christians, the Jews in particular, any follower of desire in his religion; 9129-9134) and al-Tabari's choice with the editor's note; in 4:28 the lightening and the weakness of man (9135-9139); in 4:29 the prohibition on consuming one another's property (9140-9142), the editor's notes on the printed text's changes to a transmitter's name, and the opening of the view on food and its abrogation by a verse of sura 24; cross-links to 6:71, 6:14, 61:8, 9:32, 68:9 and 24:61; cited as {cite:tafsir-tabari-shakir-v08 p. 217}, the next stop is p. 218 in 4:29. Verses touched: 4:26, 4:27, 4:28, 4:29. Statements are given as transmitted, with al-Tabari's choices and the editor's notes attributed to them and no museum verdict.
- Passages 4:25 closed and 4:26 begun (volume 8, pp. 202-209, reports 9101-9128): in 4:25 the rest of the view that protection means marriage (9101-9107, with the report from Ibn Abbas and the editor's note on its verb), the clause on half the penalty with the lashes and exile as al-Tabari states them and Qatada's different report (9108-9109, 9108 cut in the sources), the meaning of the word for hardship in the clause permitting this for one who fears it, the view that it is fornication (9110-9120) and al-Tabari's choice covering religion and body, and the clause on refraining from marrying slave women, forgiveness and mercy (9121-9128); in 4:26 the opening clause on God's will to explain the lawful and the forbidden, the paths of those before, repentance, His knowledge and wisdom, and the start of the grammarians' discussion (with a link to 42:15). Verses linked: 4:25, 4:26, 3:118, 42:15. The statements are given as transmitted, the choices and arguments are al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings are the editor's, and the museum gives no verdict. Ar, en and ur, citing tafsir-tabari-shakir-v08 pp. 202-209. Files: passage/quran-4-25 and passage/quran-4-26.
- Passage 4:25 continued (volume 8, pp. 194-201, reports 9076-9101): the rest of the reports on the description of chaste women who are neither open fornicators nor takers of lovers (9076-9083, with the editor's note on the unpointed word of 9076), the readers' dispute over the verb for protection in the clause on the penalty and al-Tabari's choice, the hadith on flogging and selling a slave woman (9084-9087, with the editor's notes and his remark on reproach), the objection and al-Tabari's reply and his choice of reading, the view that protection means Islam (9088-9099, with reports from Ibn Mas'ud, al-Shabi, Ibrahim, al-Zuhri on Umar, al-Suddi and others and the editor's notes on the names in 9089-9090), and the start of the view that it means marriage (9100, with 9101 cut at the end of p. 201). The statements are given as transmitted, the choices and arguments are al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings are the editor's, and the museum gives no verdict. Ar, en and ur, citing tafsir-tabari-shakir-v08 pp. 194-201. File: passage/quran-4-25.
- Passage 4:25 continued (volume 8, pp. 186-193, reports 9062-9075): the rest of the explanation of the believing free women with the reports from Ibn Abbas, Mujahid, al-Suddi, Sa'id ibn Jubayr, Ibn Zayd and al-Hasan (9062-9068), the readers' differences over the vowel of the word for protected women and al-Tabari's choice, the dispute over marrying slave women of the People of the Book with the reports of Mujahid and of al-Awza'i, Sa'id ibn Abd al-Aziz, Malik ibn Anas and others (9069-9072), the Iraqi view and Abu Hanifa's argument from 5:5, the editor's notes on the narrators of 9071, al-Tabari's choice, the clause on God's knowledge of faith as a postposed clause, the clause on marrying with the families' leave and giving the dowers (9073), and the start of the explanation of the chaste women who are neither open fornicators nor takers of lovers (9074-9075, with the report on the Jahiliyya, 6:151). Verses linked: 4:25, 4:24, 5:5, 6:151. The statements are given as transmitted, the choices and arguments are al-Tabari's, the notes and gradings are the editor's, and the museum gives no verdict. - Passages 4:24 closed and 4:25 begun (volume 8, pp. 178-185, reports 9040-9061): in 4:24 the rest of the temporary-marriage reports (9040-9043), al-Tabari's choice on the enjoyment and the editor's notes on report 9044 (the Sabra hadith, Ahmad, Muslim, Ibn Hajar on abrogation, as the editor states them), then the closing clause on agreement after the stipulation with four views (9045-9048), al-Tabari's choice and his explanation of God's description of Himself; in 4:25 the meaning of the word for means, the view that it is wealth and ampleness (9049-9056), the view that it is desire with reports on a free man marrying a slave woman (9057-9061), al-Tabari's choice and the editor's notes, and the start of his explanation of the clause on believing free women, cut mid-sentence at the end of p. 185. - Passages 3:195 closed (volume 7, pp. 489-492, report 8370), 3:196 (p. 493, reports 8371-8372), 3:197 (pp. 493-494), 3:198 (pp. 494-496, reports 8373-8375) and 3:199 begun (pp. 496-497, reports 8376-8378): the particle in the verse and the Basran and Kufan grammarians, the emigrants and the report on the poor emigrants with the editor's tracing, the readers' difference on fighting and being killed and al-Tabari's choice, the warning against being deceived by the disbelievers' travels, the reports that death is better, and the first view on who is meant in verse 199 (the Negus) with the editor's gradings. Touched: 3:195, 3:196, 3:197, 3:198, 3:199. The filings of 3:188-3:195 were spot-checked against the Tanzil numbering and agree with the page headings.

- Passages 3:193 (volume 7, pp. 480-482, reports 8361-8365), 3:194 (pp. 483-485, reports 8366) and 3:195 begun (pp. 486-488, reports 8367-8369): the views on who the caller is (the Qur'an or the Prophet) with al-Tabari's choice and the editor's chain notes, the explanation of the call and of the abrar, the three views on why the promised is asked for and al-Tabari's choice (the emigrants and followers asking for hastened help), his replies to the other views, then verse 195 begun: the answer, the Umm Salama reports on women and the emigration with the editor's tracing of them, and the sense of answering. - Passages 3:188 (volume 7, pp. 471-472, reports 8350-8353), 3:189 (p. 473), 3:190 (pp. 473-474), 3:191 (pp. 474-476, reports 8354-8355) and 3:192 (pp. 477-479, reports 8356-8360): the seventh view on verse 188 (the Jews of Khaybar, with Ka'b's message to Ibn Mas'ud) and al-Tabari's choice, then verse 189 (the answer to the claim of poverty), 190 (the argument from the heavens, earth, night and day), 191 (the remembrance in every state, the grammar question, reflection on creation and the plea) and 192 (the two views on what disgrace means, with the Anas, Ibn al-Musayyab, al-Hasan and Jabir reports as transmitted and the editor's chain notes), with the editor's notes. - Library note for Tabari volume 7 (docs/delegation/library/tafsir-tabari-shakir.md): the scan oddities (offset, header glitches, garbled quotations, inferred report numbers, cut footnotes) are recorded for later readers.
- Passages 3:187 (volume 7, pp. 463-464, reports 8330-8334) and 3:188 (pp. 465-470, reports 8335-8349): al-Tabari's choice of the ya' reading with his argument, the sense of casting the covenant behind the back (al-Sha'bi, Ibn Jurayj), the small price, then verse 188 with the six views on who is meant (hypocrites who stayed behind, the rabbis, the Jews' agreement to deny the Prophet, altering the Book, rejoicing at what God gave Abraham's family, concealing the answer to the Prophet's question with the Marwan and Ibn 'Abbas reports) and the editor's notes; also rewords two-word echoes of verse wording in the Arabic of 3:180. Library note gains a volume 7 section. Touched: 3:180, 3:187, 3:188.
- Passage 3:186 (volume 7, pp. 455-458, reports 8316-8317) and 3:187 (pp. 458-462, reports 8318-8329): the rest of al-Tabari's commentary on the testing and the harm, the two views on the occasion (Finhas, and the Ka'b ibn al-Ashraf report with the editor's notes on al-'Awali, the measure, and the helping verb), then verse 187: the covenant to make the Prophet clear and the concealment, the views on who is meant (the Jews, everyone with knowledge, or the prophets' covenant with their peoples, reports 8318-8328), al-Hasan's explanation and the two readings, with the editor's notes on manuscript and printed variants. Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary on verse 187 continues on p. 463.
- Passages 3:180-186 (volume 7, pp. 440-454, reports 8299-8315): the close of the views on the collaring (Mujahid, al-Tabari's reference to his opening choice), the inheritance of the heavens and the earth, 3:181 (the Finhas and Abu Bakr reports 8300-8309, the readings of the verb for recording and al-Tabari's choice, how the killing of prophets is ascribed to later Jews, and the burning punishment), 3:182 (God's justice), 3:183 (the offering consumed by fire, reports 8310-8311), 3:184 (consoling the Prophet, the scriptures, the Book and the illuminating, reports 8312-8313), 3:185 (the end of every soul, the enjoyment of deception, reports 8314-8315 with the editor's gradings of the Abu Hurayra hadith) and the opening of 3:186, with the editor's notes on manuscript gaps and source tracing. Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary on verse 186 continues on p. 455.
- Passage 3:180 (volume 7, pp. 431-439, reports 8278-8298): the close of the grammarians' discussion and al-Tabari's choice of the reading with ta', the question of what the miserliness is (withheld zakat or the Jewish concealment), the reports on the collaring with a serpent (Abu Malik al-'Abdi, Bahz ibn Hakim from his grandfather, Ibn Mas'ud in stopped and raised forms), the collar of fire, concealment by the rabbis and being charged to bring it, with the editor's long notes on narrators, corrected names and source tracing. Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary on verse 180 continues on p. 440.
- Passages 3:178-180 (volume 7, pp. 422-430, reports 8267-8277): the reciters' difference on the opening verb of 3:178 with al-Tabari's choice and the report of Ibn Mas'ud on death; 3:179 (the separation of believer from hypocrite, the two views, and the discussion of knowledge of the unseen with al-Tabari's choice); and the beginning of 3:180 (the readings and the Kufan and Basran grammarians' views, with the editor's long note on the disordered Basran sentence). Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary on verse 180 continues on p. 431.
- Passages 3:173-178 (volume 7, pp. 413-421, reports 8251-8266): al-Tabari's argument for the Hamra' al-Asad reading of 3:173, then 3:174 (the return with well-being and trade profit), 3:175 (Satan's frightening through his allies, the Arabic-usage question and the command to fear God alone), 3:176 and 3:177 (the hypocrites' rush into disbelief and their purchase of disbelief) and the opening of 3:178 (the meaning of respite), with the editor's textual and lexical notes. Poetry described, not reproduced; given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary on verse 178 continues on p. 422.
- Passages 3:172-173 (volume 7, pp. 404-412, reports 8242-8250): the close of 3:172 and the editor's tracing of the 'A'isha report, then 3:173 with al-Tabari's reading of the wording, the two views on when the warning was spoken (the pursuit to Hamra' al-Asad or the expedition of Badr al-Sughra), the reports of Ibn Ishaq (Ma'bad al-Khuza'i and the 'Abd al-Qays party), al-Suddi, Ibn 'Abbas, Qatada, Mujahid, Ibn Jurayj and 'Ikrima, the editor's lexical and textual notes, and al-Tabari's choice. Verses described, not reproduced; given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary continues on p. 413.
- Passages 3:169-172 (volume 7, pp. 395-403, reports 8225-8241): the close of the report on the martyrs' message, the grammar of the word for rejoicing, the reports on being glad for those not yet joined (3:170), the reciters' difference on the last particle of 3:171 with al-Tabari's choice, and the Hamra' al-Asad pursuit after Uhud for 3:172 (reports of Ibn Ishaq, Qatada, al-Suddi, Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn Jurayj and 'A'isha), with the editor's notes on narrators and corrections. Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary on verse 172 continues on p. 404.
- Passage 3:169 (volume 7, pp. 386-394, reports 8206-8224): the reports on the spirits of the martyrs with God (Ibn Mas'ud through five chains, Ibn 'Abbas on the river Bariq, Jabir on his father's reviving, Qatada, al-Rabi', Ibn Jurayj, Ibn Ishaq, al-Dahhak, al-Suddi, al-Hasan) and Anas ibn Malik's report on Bi'r Ma'una, with the editor's long notes on narrators, source tracing and corrections to the printed text; report 8225 begins at the top of p. 394. Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary continues on p. 395.
- Passages 3:166 to 3:170 (volume 7, pp. 377-385, reports 8192-8205): 3:166 on what befell at Uhud by God's leave and the distinguishing of believers and hypocrites; 3:167 on 'Abdallah ibn Ubayy and those who turned back from Uhud (Ibn Ishaq's long report from al-Zuhri and others, al-Suddi, 'Ikrima, Mujahid) and the two views on the meaning of repelling (increase numbers or keep watch), with the editor's notes on narrators; 3:168 on those who said of their brothers that had they obeyed they would not have been killed, with the identification with Ibn Ubayy and the editor's note on the end of a part of the old manuscript division; 3:169 begun with the report of the martyrs' spirits in green birds (Ibn 'Abbas) and the editor's long tracing of the hadith. Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the commentary continues on p. 386.
- Passages 3:163, 3:164 and 3:165 (volume 7, pp. 367-376, reports 8172-8191): 3:163 on the differing ranks of those who followed God's pleasure and those who incurred His anger (Ibn Ishaq, Ibn 'Abbas, Mujahid, al-Suddi) with the tiers expression and the editor's note on the poetic witness (described, not quoted); 3:164 on the favour of sending a messenger from among the believers (Qatada, with the editor's note on the people of Harura' as the Kharijites, and Ibn Ishaq); 3:165 on the calamity at Uhud and its cause, the two views (disobeying the advice to stay in Medina; taking ransom for the captives of Badr) with the reports of Qatada, 'Ikrima, al-Hasan, Ibn Jurayj, al-Suddi, Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn Ishaq, al-Dahhak, 'Ubayda al-Salmani and 'Ali, and the editor's textual notes. Given as transmitted without a museum verdict; the OCR of p. 376 ends mid-report; the commentary continues with 3:166-167 on p. 377.
