- Passage 2:197 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 148-153): his own choice on "no disputing in the hajj" (the dispute over the hajj time is void; labelled his) with his arguments against the wrangling and abuse readings, and the hadith of Abu Hurayra (whoever performs hajj without rafath or fusuq) by its many routes, with the editor's notes on narrators and printing errors, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:197 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 143-148): the reports on "no disputing in the hajj" (wrangling until one angers a companion, abuse, rivalry over whose hajj is more complete, disputes over the day or the standing places, and Mujahid's account that it means the hajj time is settled and the postponement (nasi') practice void, with the 'Ikrima, Ibn 'Umar, Ibn Zayd, al-Qurazi and al-Qasim ibn Muhammad reports), with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:197 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 138-143): the last views on "nor fusuq" (abuse, slaughter for idols, nicknames), his own choice that it means what is forbidden specifically in ihram (labelled his) with his argument, and the start of "no disputing in the hajj" (Ibn Mas'ud, Ibn 'Abbas, 'Ata', Sa'id ibn Jubayr, Mujahid and others), with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:197 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 133-138): his own choice that the ban on rafath covers all its senses (labelled his), and the start of "nor fusuq" with the views that it means all disobedience (Ibn 'Abbas, 'Ata', Mujahid and others), the acts forbidden in ihram (Ibn 'Umar), or abuse (Ibn 'Umar, al-Dahhak, al-Suddi and others), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:197 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 128-133): the rest of the reports on "no rafath", first those taking it as allusion to intercourse (i'raba), then those taking it as intercourse itself (Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn 'Umar, Ibn Mas'ud, al-Hasan, 'Ata', Mujahid, Qatada, Sa'id ibn Jubayr, al-Suddi, Ibn Zayd and others), with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:197 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 123-128): the end of the "making the hajj binding" question (ihram, with his own reasoning from three possibilities, labelled his) and the start of "no rafath" (Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn 'Umar, 'Ata', Tawus, al-Qurazi, Ibn al-Zubayr), with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:197 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 118-123): the reports on 'umra in and outside the months of hajj (Ibn Mas'ud, al-Qasim ibn Muhammad, Ibn Sirin, Ibn 'Umar), his own choice that the hajj months are two months and ten days of the third and his answer about the plural "months" (labelled his), and the start of the commentary on "whoever makes the hajj binding on himself in them" (the talbiya or the ihram; Ibn 'Umar, Ata', Mujahid, Tawus, al-Thawri, Ibrahim, al-Qasim, Ibn 'Abbas, al-Hasan, Qatada, al-Dahhak), with the editor's notes on narrators and a printing error, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 closes its volume 4 commentary (p. 114, "fear God and know that God is severe in punishment"), and passage 2:197 gains its first commentary from al-Tabari (pp. 114-118): the grammar of "the hajj is in known months", the two views on the months (Shawwal, Dhu al-Qa'da and ten days of Dhu al-Hijja, or all of Dhu al-Hijja; Ibn Mas'ud, Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn 'Umar, Mujahid, 'Ata' and others) and al-Tabari's explanation of what the second group meant, with the editor's note on a dropped report number, in Arabic, English and Urdu; the source al-Tabari (Shakir edition, vol. 4) is added to the data record of 2:197.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 108-113): the remaining interpretations of "ten complete" and his own choice (labelled his), and the commentary on "that is for one whose family is not present at the Sacred Mosque": who counts as present (the people of the Haram only; those within the miqats; those near, such as 'Arafa; Ibn 'Abbas, Mujahid, Tawus, Makhul, 'Ata', al-Zuhri, Ibn Zayd), al-Tabari's own choice by the distance at which prayer is shortened, and his reason why tamattu' does not apply to the Makkan (labelled his), with the editor's note on variant wording in other books, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 103-108): when the three days' fast may begin (Ata', Ikrima, Ibn 'Umar, Ibn 'Abbas, Mujahid), his own choice and his arguments against fasting before the hajj ihram (labelled his, with the editor's note on a printing error), the seven days on return and the view that they may be fasted on the road (Mujahid, Ata', Ibrahim), and the first interpretations of "ten complete", in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 97-102): the last day of the three days' fast (the day of 'Arafa, or the days of al-Tashriq), the reasoning of each side as reported by al-Tabari, the editor's notes on the chains and on the wording "no concession was given", and the first positions on when the fast may begin (the months of hajj or the ten of Dhu al-Hijja only), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 91-96): the views on who is a mutamatti' (Ibn 'Umar, 'Ata', Ibn Abi Layla, Sa'id ibn al-Musayyab, Ibn 'Abbas), al-Tabari's own choice linking the verse to the one who was prevented (labelled his, with his allowance that others can also be mutamatti'), and the first part of the commentary on the three days' fast in the hajj, with the positions of 'Ali, Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn 'Umar, 'Urwa, al-Hakam, Sa'id ibn Jubayr, 'Amir, al-Hasan, Ibrahim, Mujahid, 'Ata' and Qatada, and the editor's note on one narrator, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 86-91): the meaning of "when you are safe" (fear of the enemy, al-Tabari's choice, labelled his, or recovery from illness), and the first positions on which tamattu' the verse means: the prevented one who then performs 'umra the next year (Ibn al-Zubayr, 'Alqama, Qatada, Ibrahim, 'Ali), both the prevented and the not prevented (Ibn 'Abbas), annulling hajj into 'umra (al-Suddi), and the 'umra in the months of hajj (Mujahid), with the editor's notes on a printing error and an unusual word, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 81-86): the reasoning behind each position on where the ransom is paid, his own choice that the place is unspecified (labelled his, with his stepdaughter analogy), the disagreement on whether the one paying may eat from the sacrifice (Ata', Mujahid, Tawus, Ibn 'Umar, Ibn Abi Layla, Hammad, al-Hasan) and his own reasoning, and the opening of the commentary on "when you are safe" (Alqama), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 77-81): the close of his argument on the ransom for shaving, and the positions on where the ransom is paid (Makka for blood and food, or anywhere, with the report of 'Ali nursing al-Husayn at al-Suqya, three versions, and al-Tabari's reading of it), each attributed (al-Hasan, Tawus, 'Ata', Mujahid, Ibrahim), with the editor's notes on narrators and a printing error, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 72-77): the ten-days and ten-poor view (al-Hasan, 'Ikrima) and its analogy to the tamattu' fast, the view of the sheep valued in food (Sa'id ibn Jubayr, Mujahid), the view that the person is free to choose among the three (Mujahid, 'Ata', 'Amr ibn Dinar, Ibn 'Abbas, 'Ikrima), and al-Tabari's own choice of free choice with his arguments against the other views (labelled his), with the editor's notes on the unidentified Ibn Abi 'Imran, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 67-72): the last routes of the hadith of Ka'b ibn 'Ujra (3356-3359) with the editor's notes, and the first positions on the measure of the ransom for shaving (three days' fasting, three sa' among six poor, or a sheep, as attributed to Abu Malik, 'Ata', Mujahid, Ibrahim, al-Suddi, al-Rabi', Sa'id ibn Jubayr, 'Ali, Muhammad ibn Ka'b and 'Alqama) and the opening of the view of ten days and ten poor, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 63-67): more routes of the hadith of Ka'b ibn 'Ujra (3344-3355), including versions that name a sheep and versions that say he had nothing to sacrifice, with the editor's notes on broken and unnamed links in the chains, the omission of Mujahid and of Ibn Abi Layla in some Muwatta narrations, and the identification of narrators, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 58-62): ten routes of the hadith of Ka'b ibn 'Ujra (lice at al-Hudaybiyya, shaving, and the choice of three days' fasting, feeding six poor or slaughtering), al-Tabari's inference from the order of events that the ransom follows the shaving (labelled his), and the editor's notes on narrators, sources, units of measure and printing errors, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 54-58) on the ransom for illness or harm to the head: whether the ransom comes before or after shaving (al-Hasan, 'Alqama, Mujahid, Qatada, Ibn Shihab, Ibn 'Umar, 'Ali, Ibn 'Abbas), the first of the 28 reports of the hadith of Ka'b ibn 'Ujra with the editor's note on its mursal chain and a doubt about one printed chain, and al-Tabari's account of which illnesses and harms the verse covers, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 49-54): reports on those prevented during 'umra (Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn 'Umar, Ibn Shihab), al-Tabari's own choice that every prevented person in ihram leaves it when the hady is slaughtered where he was held (labelled his, with the Hudaybiyya basis and his arguments against the opposing views), the report of al-Hajjaj ibn 'Amr with the editor's notes on its sources, and the start of his commentary on the ransom for illness or harm to the head, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 44-49): the view that the hady's place is the Haram only, with the Najiya ibn Jundab report and the editor's notes on its narrators; the third reading of the verse as prevention that leaves the hajj missed (no prevention for 'umra, or none by an enemy today, in the words of Ibn 'Abbas and 'A'isha); and the rulings attributed to Ibn 'Umar on illness, on the man Ibn Hizaba, on missing 'Arafa and on prevention by an enemy, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 40-44): the argument that the "place" of the hady is where it is slaughtered (with the Barira meat report and the editor's note on its wording), the view that its place is the Haram only (Ibn Mas'ud's ruling for the man stung at Dhat al-Shuquq, with the editor's note on the man's name), and the reports of Ibn 'Abbas, 'Ali, 'Ata' and al-Suddi, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 35-39): the reading of "al-hady" and why it is so named, his meaning of not shaving until the hady reaches its place (with the editor's note on Ibn Kathir's different reading of the clause), the view that for prevention by an enemy the place is where one was held (Malik, Ibn 'Umar, 'Ata'), and the Hudaybiyya reports including the Umm Salama report, with the editor's notes on the very weak chain of one of them, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 29-34): the two positions on what "what is easy of the hady" is, a sheep ('Ali, Ibn 'Abbas, Malik, 'Ata') or a camel or cow (Ibn 'Umar, 'A'isha, Mujahid, Tawus, 'Urwa), each attributed, then al-Tabari's own choice of the sheep and his answer to the objection (labelled his), and his note on the grammar of "ma", in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 25-29): Malik's position on the one prevented by an enemy and by illness, with the ground his followers give, al-Tabari's own choice on the meaning of ihsar and his argument from the verse's next clause (labelled his), and the first reports on "what is easy of the hady" as a sheep (Ibn 'Abbas, al-Hasan, Qatada, 'Ata', al-Suddi, 'Alqama, al-Qasim ibn Muhammad, 'Ikrima, Ibrahim), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 21-24): the two positions on what "if you are prevented" (ihsar) covers, any restraint including illness (Mujahid, 'Ata', Qatada, 'Urwa, Ibrahim, Ibn 'Abbas) or only restraint by an enemy (Ibn 'Abbas through Mujahid, 'Ata' and Tawus, and Malik's report on al-Hudaybiyya), with the linguistic grounds each side gives, each attributed and with no verdict, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 16-20): the reports quoted for and against the umra being obligatory (Abu al-Muntafiq, Abu Razin al-'Uqayli, Abu Qilaba, Jabir, Abu Salih al-Hanafi), the editor's notes disagreeing with al-Tabari on one chain and quoting Ahmad and al-Shafi'i, al-Tabari's reasoning and his own conclusion that the umra is voluntary and the verse bids completion after entering them (labelled as his), and his account of the verse's setting at al-Hudaybiyya, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:196 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 11-15): the reports of Sa'id ibn Jubayr, 'Ata', al-Suddi, 'Ali and Ibn Mas'ud on whether the umra is obligatory or voluntary, with the editor's notes on weak chains and the irregular reading, the argument of those who read the umra in the nominative, and al-Tabari's own choice of the accusative reading and of the reading of Ibn Mas'ud's view, labelled as his, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Volume 3 of al-Tabari (Shakir edition) is finished: the commentary from 2:124 to 2:195 has been read and cited by printed page. The library record `docs/delegation/library/tafsir-tabari-shakir.md` now records the end of volume 3 and the page offset of volume 4 (zero: markers equal printed pages; the commentary on 2:196 starts on printed p. 7 and runs to p. 600), with an audit of its page headers. Passage 2:196 gains the first part of al-Tabari's commentary from volume 4 (pp. 7-11): the views he records on what "complete the hajj and the umra" means (attributed to Ibn 'Abbas, 'Alqama, Ibrahim, 'Ali, Sa'id ibn Jubayr, Tawus, Qatada, al-Qasim ibn Muhammad, Sufyan, Ibn Zayd) and the reports on whether the umra is obligatory (al-Sha'bi, Abu Burda, Masruq, 'Ali ibn al-Husayn), with no verdict; 2:196 now cites volume 4 as a source, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:195 gains the rest of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 590-595): the second report of Abu Ayyub al-Ansari at Constantinople with the editor's dating and chain notes, al-Tabari's own choice and reading of "do not throw yourselves into destruction" (labelled his), the grammar of the ba' and of tahluka, and "and do good" with the reports of a Companion, 'Ikrima and Ibn Zayd, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:195 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 586-590): the further reports that "do not throw yourselves into destruction" means leaving off spending (Qatada, al-Suddi, 'Ikrima, al-Hasan, 'Ata', Ibn 'Abbas, al-Dahhak), the view that it means going out without provision (Ibn Zayd), the view that it means despairing of repentance (al-Bara' ibn 'Azib, 'Ubayda al-Salmani), and the view that it means abandoning jihad, with Abu Ayyub al-Ansari's account at Constantinople, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:194 and 2:195 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 580-585): the end of 2:194, with his own choice of the Mujahid view that the verse is Medinan, a gap in the text noted by the editor and his bracketed reconstruction of the two senses of i'tada, and for 2:195 the first reports on "spend in the way of God and do not throw yourselves into destruction" (Hudhayfa, Ibn 'Abbas, 'Ikrima, Muhammad ibn Ka'b, 'Amir, Mujahid, Qatada); 2:195 now cites al-Tabari (Shakir edition, volume 3) as a source, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:194 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 575-580): the reports on the sacred month and the 'umra of al-Hudaybiyya and its make-up (Ibn 'Abbas, Mujahid, Qatada, Miqsam, al-Suddi, al-Dahhak, al-Rabi', 'Ata'), the view of Ibn Zayd that it was abrogated, al-Tabari's explanation of the sacred month and "the sanctities", the editor's notes on chains and an unclear report, and the two positions on the occasion of "whoever transgresses against you" (Ibn 'Abbas; Mujahid), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:193 and 2:194 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 571-575): the rest of 2:193 (the meaning of din, the report of the Prophet's command quoted by al-Rabi', the two views of "no aggression except against the wrongdoers" attributed to Qatada, al-Rabi', 'Ikrima and to Mujahid, al-Suddi, with the Basran grammarians' ellipsis question) and the start of 2:194 (the sacred month and the 'umra of al-Hudaybiyya and its make-up); 2:194 now cites al-Tabari (Shakir edition, volume 3) as a source, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:191, 2:192 and 2:193 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 566-570): the rest of 2:191 with the reports on fitna, the two readings of "do not fight them at the Sacred Mosque" and the positions he records on whether it was abrogated (Qatada, al-Rabi', Ibn Zayd on one side, Mujahid on the other), his own choice of reading, labelled as his, and the editor's notes; 2:192 ("if they desist") and 2:193 now cite al-Tabari (Shakir edition, volume 3) as a source, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:189, 2:190 and 2:191 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 561-565): the close of 2:189; for 2:190 the two positions he records on whether the verse on fighting was abrogated by al-Tawba (the reports of al-Rabi' and Ibn Zayd, and those of 'Umar ibn 'Abd al-'Aziz, Mujahid and Ibn 'Abbas), his own choice, labelled as his, and his reading; for 2:191 his reading of "kill them wherever you find them", the expulsion, and fitna with the reports of Mujahid and Qatada. 2:190 and 2:191 now cite al-Tabari (Shakir edition, volume 3) as a source, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:189 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 556-560): the reports on why the verse about entering houses from their backs was revealed (al-Bara', Qays ibn Hubtar, Mujahid, al-Zuhri, Qatada, al-Suddi, Ibn 'Abbas, al-Rabi', 'Ata'), the editor's notes on chains and on the hums, and al-Tabari's own interpretation, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:188 and 2:189 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 551-555): the rest of the reports on 2:188 (Qatada, al-Suddi, 'Ikrima, Ibn Zayd), his account of the word idla' and the two grammatical readings, and for 2:189 the reports on the crescents as times (Qatada, Ibn 'Abbas, 'Ali and others) with the editor's notes on chains, and his own interpretation; 2:189 now cites al-Tabari (Shakir edition, volume 3) as a source, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:187 and 2:188 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 546-550): the close of the retreat matter, his reading of "the limits of God" and "thus does God make clear" (2:187), and for 2:188 his reading of consuming wealth by falsehood and taking it to the rulers, with the reports of Ibn 'Abbas, Mujahid and Qatada and the editor's notes; 2:188 now cites al-Tabari (Shakir edition, volume 3) as a source, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 541-545): the reports of al-Dahhak, al-Rabi', Qatada, al-Suddi, Mujahid, Ibn 'Abbas and 'Ata' on what "contact" means during retreat, the view of Malik and Ibn Zayd that it covers every kind of contact, al-Tabari's own choice, and 'A'isha's reports of combing the Prophet's hair, with the editor's notes on chains, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 536-540): the reports on continuous fasting (wisal) from Ibn 'Umar, Abu Sa'id and the umm walad of Hatib, with the editor's notes on chains, al-Tabari's reading of "complete the fast to the night", and the start of the commentary on "do not have contact with them while you are in retreat" with the first reports of Ibn 'Abbas and 'Ata', in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 531-535): the end of his argument on the dawn thread, his explanation of the word fajr, the reports on breaking the fast at sunset from 'Umar and Ibn Abi Awfa, the reports of Abu al-'Aliya and 'A'isha on continuous fasting, and his answer on those who practised it, each attributed, with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 527-531): the reports of Abu Hurayra, Abu Umama and Bilal on drinking when the prayer was called, with the editor's notes on chains, then al-Tabari's own choice of the meaning of the white and black thread and his argument against the view that eating may continue to sunrise, with Ibn Zayd's explanation, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 522-526): the reports from 'Ali and the grounds he gives for the view that the pre-dawn meal may run to the sun's rising, and the reports of Hudhayfa about the Prophet's pre-dawn meal, each attributed, with the editor's notes on chains and on Ibn Hajar's and al-Nasa'i's remarks, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 517-521): the second view on the white and black thread and the reports from Hudhayfa, 'Ali, Ibn Mas'ud and Abu Bakr about the pre-dawn meal, each attributed to the Companion named, with the editor's notes on chains, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 512-516): the reports of 'Adi ibn Hatim and Sahl ibn Sa'd on the white and black thread, and the reports that the true dawn is the spreading one and not the false dawn (Abu Mijlaz, Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn Thawban, Samura ibn Jundub), with the editor's notes on chains and on text restored from Ibn Kathir, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 507-511): the remaining views and readings on "seek what God has written for you", his choice (labelled as his), and the first views on the white and black thread with the editor's note quoting the report of 'Adi ibn Hatim, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 504-507): the meaning of "mubashara" as reported from named commentators, and the differing views on "seek what God has written for you" (the child, intercourse, the Night of Qadr), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 499-503): further reports on the occasion of the verse (Mujahid, 'Ikrima, Qatada, al-Suddi, 'Ata', Ibn Hibban), with the editor's notes on chains and wording, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 494-498): the reports on the occasion of the verse (Ibn Abi Layla, Mu'adh, al-Bara', Ibn 'Abbas, Ka'b ibn Malik and others) with the editor's notes on chains and on the differing name of the Ansari, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:187 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 488-493): the senses of "a garment for you" and the two kinds of "betrayal" mentioned in the verse, with the report of Ibn Abi Layla; the prose notes the editor's mention that the text has a gap at one point, each view attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:186-187 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 484-488): the close of 2:186 (the senses of "respond to Me", "believe in Me", and his two answers on unanswered prayer, with the editor's note on the supporting report's chain) and the start of 2:187 ("rafath"), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:185-186 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 477-483): the close of 2:185 (his grammatical choice on "and that you complete the number", the takbir at the end of the fast as reported from named commentators, and "that you may give thanks"), and the start of 2:186 ("I am near") with the differing reports of what the verse was revealed about, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:185 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 472-477): the objection from the report "fasting on a journey is no part of righteousness" and his answer, the grammar of "or on a journey", "God wants ease for you" with the reports of Ibn 'Abbas and others, and the start of "that you complete the number", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:185 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 467-472): further reports that breaking the fast on a journey is a concession, al-Tabari's own choice and his stated reasons (labelled as his), and the Hamza al-Aslami reports, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:185 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 462-467): the view that fasting on a journey must be made up and its supporting report, and the opposing view that breaking the fast on a journey is a concession (reports of 'Umar ibn 'Abd al-'Aziz, 'Umar ibn al-Khattab, Anas and others), each attributed, with the editor's notes on chains, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:185 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 458-462): the views on which illness permits breaking the fast (including a view attributed to al-Shafi'i), al-Tabari's choice, the grammar of "other days", and the first view that breaking the fast on a journey is obligatory, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:185 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 452-458): more reports on the traveller, the view attributed to Abu Hanifa and his companions on madness, al-Tabari's reasons for rejecting two readings of "witnessing the month" and choosing the third, and the first views on which illness permits breaking the fast, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:185 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 447-452): the sending down of the Qur'an in Ramadan, the meanings of guidance, proofs and criterion, and the differing views on "witnessing the month" for residents and travellers, each view attributed to named transmitters, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:184-185 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 442-447): the close of 2:184 ("whoever volunteers good", "that you fast is better"), and the start of 2:185 (the word "month", the name Ramadan, and reports on the Qur'an being sent down on the Night of Qadr), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:184 (volume 3, pp. 427-437): replaced an unsourced remark about the schools with a statement limited to what the cited pages report from Ibn 'Abbas and Ibn 'Umar, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:184 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 436-442): his answer on the pregnant and nursing woman (labelled as his own choice, with the contrary reports noted), the meaning of "fidya", the readings of "fidya of a poor person's food" and his preferences, the differing amounts of food, and the first views on "whoever volunteers good", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:184 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 431-436): the remaining reports on the ransom clause, including conflicting reports from Ibn 'Abbas on whether it was abrogated, al-Tabari's own choice that it was abrogated and his stated reasons, and the objection about the pregnant and nursing woman with the report of Anas al-Ka'bi, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:184 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 425-430): the view that the ransom clause was never abrogated and covers those who become unable to fast, the reports of Ibn 'Abbas, Ibn 'Umar and Ibn al-Musayyab on the old, pregnant and nursing, and the "yutawwaqunahu" reading, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:184 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 419-425): the reports that the option to feed instead of fasting was abrogated by "whoever witnesses the month", the view that it was a ruling for the able old man and woman, and Ibn 'Abbas on the old, pregnant and nursing, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:184 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 413-419): the competing views on the "limited days" (three days a month versus Ramadan), his choice, the make-up days for the ill and travelling, the reading of "yutiqunahu", and the first reports on the early option to feed instead of fasting, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:182-184 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 407-413): the close of 2:182, the meaning of fasting and the competing views on who "those before you" are (Christians, People of the Book, all people), his choice, and the start of 2:184, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:182 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 401-407): the further views on setting right a partial bequest, his choice and his reason, the readings, and the meanings of "janaf" and "ithm", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:180, 2:181 and 2:182 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 395-401): his choice that a bequest binds from any amount, who bears the sin of altering a bequest, and the first views on setting right a partial bequest, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:180 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 390-395): the reports of those who held the bequest wholly abrogated, what "good" means, and the differing views on the amount of wealth at which a bequest binds, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:180 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 385-390): his answer that the bequest is obligatory for non-heirs, and the three positions among scholars on whether the verse is abrogated by the inheritance verses, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:178, 2:179 and 2:180 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 380-384): the close of 2:178 with his choice on the painful punishment, 2:179 on life in retaliation, and the opening of 2:180 on the bequest, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:178 gains the rest of al-Tabari's commentary on "an alleviation and a mercy" and on "whoever transgresses after that" (volume 3, pp. 373-379): the reports on blood-money in earlier communities and the two views on the painful punishment, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:178 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 367-373): the reports on "whoever is pardoned something by his brother", the view that it means a remainder of the blood-money, his choice, the meaning of the reasonable pursuit and good payment, the grammar of the nominative, and the opening on "an alleviation and a mercy", attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:178 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 361-367): the reports on 'Ali and others about setting off blood-money, his reasoning for which reading of the verse stands and why, the meaning of "prescribed" and "qisas", and the first views on pardon and "reasonable pursuit", attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:177 and 2:178 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 356-361): the close of 2:177 ("those are the ones who were true") and, for 2:178, what "prescribed" means, the view that qisas offsets blood-money, and the interpreters' differing reports on its occasion, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:177 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 349-355): the reports on "hardship and affliction", the grammarians' views on the two words and his preference, why "the patient" is in the accusative as praise (and his rejection of coordination with "those who ask"), and "in time of stress", attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:177 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 344-349): giving to kin, the wayfarer, those who ask, freeing slaves, the two views on a due beyond zakat, keeping covenants, and the first reports on "hardship and affliction", attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:177 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 337-343): the report that Jews and Christians are addressed and his choice, how "righteousness is whoever believes" is read, and giving wealth "for love of it" with the reports on a due beyond zakat, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:175, 2:176 and 2:177 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 331-337): the views on "how patient they are with the Fire" and his choice, what "that" refers to in 2:176 and who differ about the Book, and the opening of 2:177 (righteousness is not turning the face east or west), attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:173, 2:174 and 2:175 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 324-330): the rest of 2:173 with his choice on "neither desiring nor transgressing" and a note that the editor judges part of the printed text corrupt, 2:174 on those who conceal the Book and what they "eat", and the opening of 2:175, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:173 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 318-324): the readings of "al-mayta", the meaning of "dedicated to other than God", and the first two views on "neither desiring nor transgressing" (the rebel and highway robber, and seeking or exceeding the forbidden), attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:171, 2:172 and 2:173 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 313-318): the close of 2:171 with his chosen reading of the likeness and the editor's footnote on it, the whole of 2:172, and the opening of 2:173 (the single particle "innama"), attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:170 and 2:171 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 307-312): the close of 2:170, and for 2:171 the views on the likeness of the one who shouts (the beast, the shepherd, the echo) with his grammatical explanation, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:168, 2:169 and 2:170 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 300-306): the lawful and good foods and the views on "the footsteps of Satan", evil and indecency and saying about God what is not known, and the opening of 2:170 with the two readings of the pronoun and the report of its occasion, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:167 gains the rest of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 293-299): the return wished for, the two views on how deeds become regrets and his choice, and that they do not leave the Fire, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Library note on al-Tabari (Shakir) records a page-header audit of volumes 2 and 3: the only out-of-order scan pages are volume 3 markers 279-282 (printed 281, 282, 279, 280); every other marker equals its printed page, so existing citations stand.
- Passages 2:166 and 2:167 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 287-293): who the followed and the followers are, the meanings given to the "ties" that are cut and his choice, and the opening of 2:167, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:165 gains al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, printed pp. 279-286): the two views on the "equals", the answer on "as they love God", and the readers' differences on "if those who did wrong could see" with his chosen reading, in Arabic, English and Urdu. Passage 2:164 loses a note wrongly saying printed pp. 279-280 were missing: the scan holds them out of order (as scan pages 281-282) and they are now read. Note for later work: in this volume's scan, scan page numbers and printed numbers can differ; citations use the printed number.
- Passage 2:164 gains the rest of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 274-278): the creatures, the turning of the winds, the clouds, why the argument holds against those who deny a creator, and the list of benefits, in Arabic, English and Urdu. It records that printed pages 279 and 280 are absent from the available scan and have not been read.
- Passage 2:159 now says plainly that al-Suddi's report no. 2374m is cut short in the printed text and that the editor notes a passage dropped by the copyist. Passage 2:164 gains al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 269-274): the reports on the occasion of revelation and his reading of them, creation as thing or quality, night and day, the ships, and the rain, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:162, 2:163 and 2:164 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 264-269): the end of 2:162, the two views on the meaning of God's oneness in 2:163, and the argument of 2:164 with the first reports on its occasion of revelation, attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:160, 2:161 and 2:162 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 259-264): the exception for those who repent and make clear (and whom he names), who is meant by the curse of "all people" and his choice, and the abiding without lightening, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:159 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 253-258): the meaning of "curse" as being put far from God's mercy, the differing views on who "the cursers" are (animals, angels and believers, others), and his choice and reasons, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:158 (end) and 2:159 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 247-252): the readings and meaning of "whoever volunteers good", and "those who conceal what We sent down" with the reports on who is meant and the general application he draws, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:158 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 241-246): the scholars' positions on one who leaves the running between Safa and Marwa (Malik, al-Shafi'i, al-Thawri and the Hanafi school, 'Ata', Ibn 'Abbas, Anas, Mujahid), his own choice and reasons, and his reply to the variant reading "not to go round them", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:158 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 235-240): the report of 'A'isha to 'Urwa and the Ansar reports on Manat, his own view that the running is among God's signs, and the three positions of scholars on one who leaves it (obligatory, compensated by a sacrifice, or voluntary), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:158 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 230-235): the answer on "no blame in going round them" and the early reports (al-Sha'bi, Anas, Ibn 'Abbas, Mujahid, Ibn Zayd) on the idols and the Muslims' reluctance, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:158 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 226-230): Safa and Marwa as the two mountains, "signs of God", the senses of hajj and 'umra, and the question raised by "no blame in going round them", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:155 (end), 2:156, 2:157 and 2:158 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 220-225): the trials and the good news to the patient, the formula of return, blessings and mercy, and the words Safa and Marwa, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:153 (end), 2:154 and 2:155 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 214-219): patience and prayer, the martyrs as "alive" and what he says was singled out for them, and the trials announced in 2:155, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:151 (end), 2:152 and 2:153 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 208-213): how "as We sent among you a messenger" is joined, the meaning of reciting, purifying, the Book and wisdom, "remember Me and I will remember you", gratitude, and "seek help through patience and prayer", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:150 (end) and 2:151 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 204-208): his reading of the exception "except those who do wrong" and the readings he rejects, "do not fear them but fear Me", completing the favour, and the link to Abraham's supplication, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:150 gains an al-Tabari commentary section (volume 3, pp. 199-203): who "the people" and "those who do wrong" are, what argument each is said to have had against the Prophet and the believers over the qibla, and the meaning of "hujja", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:148 (end) and 2:149 gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 194-198): the readings of "wijha" and his choice, "race to the good", "God will bring you all together", and the repeated command to face the Sacred Mosque, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:146 (end), 2:147 and 2:148 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 188-193): what the party conceals, "the truth is from your Lord" (and his answer on whether the Prophet doubted), and "for each there is a direction", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:144 (end), 2:145 and 2:146 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 184-188): that God is not unaware, why the People of the Book would not follow the Prophet's qibla nor each other's, the warning about following their desires, and that they know it as they know their sons, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:144 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 179-183): what counts as facing the qibla, the reports on the House's door as its qibla, "wherever you are, turn your faces toward it", and "those given the Book know it is the truth", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:144 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 173-178): the reasons given for the Prophet's longing for the Ka'ba, the meaning of "shatr" (toward), and the disagreement over which part of the Sacred Mosque is meant, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:143 (end) and 2:144 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 168-173): "God would never let your faith be lost" as the prayers toward Jerusalem, "Compassionate and Merciful", and "We see the turning of your face toward the sky", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:143 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 164-168): what "it was heavy" refers to (the turning, the qibla or the prayer) and his choice, and the reports on "God would never let your faith be lost" as the prayer toward Jerusalem, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:143 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 158-163): his answer on how "that We might know" is understood and the other views he sets aside, and the meaning of "follows the Messenger" and "turns on his heels", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:143 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 154-158): the four "witnesses", the qibla as a trial (reports of Qatada, al-Suddi and 'Ata' on the reactions of hypocrites, Muslims, Jews and polytheists), and his answer to how "that We might know" is understood, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:143 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 150-154): more attributed reports on "witnesses over the people" (Noah's people, the report of the angels and messengers, and the views of al-Dahhak, al-Rabi', Ibn 'Abbas and 'Ata'), in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:143 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 145-149): "just" as the sense of "middle", and "witnesses over the people" with the reports of Noah and his people, the mound on the Day of Resurrection and the funeral reports, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:142 (end) and 2:143 (opening) gain al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 139-144): the hypocrites' objection, "to God belong the east and the west", and the meaning of "a middle community" (best, just, moderate), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:142 continues its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 135-139): further reports on how long the Prophet prayed toward Jerusalem, the views on whether that was his choice or God's obligation, and the reasons given for the objection, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:142 gains an al-Tabari commentary section (volume 3, pp. 129-134): who "the fools" are, the meaning of "qibla", and the reported durations of prayer toward Jerusalem and the reasons given for the change, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:141 gains an al-Tabari commentary section (volume 3, pp. 128-129): who "that community" refers to, and his explanation that each community has what it earned and is not asked about others, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:140 closes its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 122-127): his choice of reading "am taqulun", the argument against the claim that Abraham and the others were Jews or Christians, the views on "who is more wrong than he who conceals a testimony" (and why he takes it to concern the prophets named), and "God is not unaware of what you do", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:138 closes its al-Tabari commentary, and 2:139 and 2:140 gain their first (volume 3, pp. 117-122): the two meanings given for "the dye of God" (God's religion, or the natural disposition), "to Him we are worshippers", "do you dispute with us about God" and the opening of "or do you say that Abraham... were Jews or Christians" with the two readings, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:136 closes its al-Tabari commentary, and 2:137 and 2:138 gain their first (volume 3, pp. 112-117): Ibn Ishaq's list of Jacob's twelve sons, "if they believe in the like of what you believe in" (including a reading reported from Ibn Abbas that the readers left and al-Tabari's explanation of the likeness), "they are only in schism", "God will suffice you against them", and the opening of "the dye of God", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:135 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:136 gains its first (volume 3, pp. 107-112): his conclusion on what hanifiyya is and why it is tied to Abraham, and the commentary on "we believe in God and in what was sent down..." (the believers' answer to the Jews and Christians, "we make no distinction", the report from Ibn Abbas on a group of Jews, and who the tribes were), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:135 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 102-107): the third way of taking "the religion of Abraham" in the accusative and the views on "hanif" (the pilgrim, the follower, the one circumcised as Abraham was, the sincere, the one upright in Islam) with his reasoning for upright adherence, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:133 closes its al-Tabari commentary, and 2:134 and 2:135 gain their first (volume 3, pp. 97-102): Jacob's question to his sons and their answer, the order of Ishmael and Isaac and a reading he rejects, "that was a nation which has passed away", and the opening of "be Jews or Christians" with the report from Ibn Abbas on Ibn Suriya, each attributed to al-Tabari or the narrators, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:131 closes its al-Tabari commentary, and 2:132 and 2:133 gain their first (volume 3, pp. 92-97): the bequest of Abraham and Jacob ("I have submitted"), the grammar of the sentence, "God has chosen the religion for you", "do not die except as Muslims", and the opening of "or were you witnesses when death came to Jacob", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:129 closes its al-Tabari commentary, and 2:130 and 2:131 gain their first (volume 3, pp. 87-92): "purify them" and "the Almighty, the Wise", who turns away from the religion of Abraham (the Jews and Christians, in his reading), "made himself foolish", "We chose him in this world", "among the righteous", and "submit" with "I have submitted", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:129 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 82-87): the report from al-'Irbad ibn Sariya on "the prayer of my father Abraham" with the editor's notes on its chains, the interpreters who say the messenger is Muhammad, and the views on "the Book and the wisdom" with al-Tabari's definition of wisdom, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:128 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:129 gains its first (volume 3, pp. 77-82): the remaining views on "show us our rites" (the silent-ra' reading, the senses of "manasik"), "turn to us in mercy" and whether the two needed repentance, and the opening of "send among them a messenger" with the report "I am the prayer of my father Abraham", each attributed with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu. The citation on the closing lines of the 2:127 commentary is also corrected to pp. 72-73 after checking the top of p. 73.
- Passage 2:127 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:128 gains its first (volume 3, pp. 72-77): "the Hearing, the Knowing", "a nation submissive to You" (and his reply to the view that the Arabs alone are meant, with the editor's note on Ibn Kathir), the readers' disagreement on "show us our rites" and the views on the rites (the pilgrimage, with al-Suddi's account of Abraham's proclamation, or the places of slaughter), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:127 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 67-72): the report of Ibn Abbas through al-Bukhari's chain, the reports from 'Ali (Abraham alone raised the foundations, Hajar and Zamzam, the sakina and the Black Stone), and al-Tabari's choice that both Abraham and Ishmael raised them and both spoke "our Lord, accept from us", each attributed with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:127 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 62-67): the reports on the place and first foundation of the House with the editor's reservations on two of them, al-Tabari's refusal to fix which of the possibilities is meant for lack of a report that settles it, "our Lord, accept from us", and the views on who raised the foundations (Abraham and Ishmael together, or Abraham with Ishmael handing him the stones), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:127 gains its first al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 57-62): the sense of "foundations" and the views on whether Abraham and Ishmael founded the House or raised foundations that already existed (a house of Adam, a house sent down from heaven, a red mound at the place of the House), with Ibn Ishaq's account of Abraham bringing Hajar and Ishmael to the site, each attributed and with the editor's reservations noted, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:126 closes its al-Tabari commentary (volume 3, pp. 51-56): how the reports on the sanctity of Mecca fit together in his view, "provide its people with fruits", the disagreement over who speaks "whoever disbelieves, I shall let him enjoy a little" and how to read it, his choice, and the senses of "I shall drive him" and "wretched is the destination", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:126 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 46-51): the two sides on whether the sanctuary of Mecca was sacred from creation or made so through Abraham, with the reports each side cited and the editor's notes on their chains, and al-Tabari's own reconciling choice, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Person Ibrahim gains a short al-Tabari section (volume 3, pp. 7-36): the views on the "words" he was tested with, on "My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers" and on the station of Abraham, pointing to the passage pages 2:124-2:126 for detail, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:125 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:126 gains its first (volume 3, pp. 41-46): the senses of "those who stay" and "those who bow and prostrate", and the opening of "make this a safe land" with the views on whether the sanctuary was safe from creation and the reports cited for it, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:125 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 36-41): his reasoning for the station of Abraham, the senses of "musalla", the command to purify the House (and the two ways he gives of understanding it, with the editor's caution), and the senses of "those who circle it" and "those who stay", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:125 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 31-36): the two readings of "ittakhidhu" and what each is joined to, the views on "the station of Abraham" (the whole pilgrimage, the standing places, the sanctuary, the stone he stood on, the station in the Sacred Mosque) and his choice, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:125 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 26-31): the senses of "a resort for the people" and "a place of safety", and the opening of the commentary on "take the station of Abraham as a place of prayer" with the readers' disagreement and the report from 'Umar, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:124 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:125 gains its first (volume 3, pp. 21-26): the views on the "covenant" that does not reach the wrongdoers (leadership, no obedience owed to a wrongdoer, safety in the Hereafter, God's religion), his reading of the verse as a notice about some of Abraham's descendants, the two readings of "al-zalimin", and the start of "a resort for the people", each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:124 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 17-21): the two reports on the "words" and his remark on their chains, the sense of "he fulfilled them", "a leader for the people", Abraham's request "and of my offspring", and the first views on "My covenant does not reach the wrongdoers" (prophethood, or the covenant of leadership), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:124 continues al-Tabari's commentary (volume 3, pp. 11-16): the remaining views on the "words" with which Abraham was tested (the rites, circumcision, the star, moon, sun, fire and emigration, the supplications), his own choice (that the words may be all or some of them, with no sound report to single one out), and the two reports he mentions with the editor's notes on their chains, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:124 gains the opening of al-Tabari's commentary in volume 3 (pp. 7-11): the disagreement over the "words" with which Abraham was tested (thirty laws of Islam, ten purification practices, purification plus pilgrimage rites, or the leadership and rites dialogue), each attributed to its narrators, in Arabic, English and Urdu. A documentation line records that volume 3 page numbers equal the printed page numbers.
- Passages 2:121 (end), 2:122 and 2:123 gain al-Tabari's commentary (volume 2, pp. 571-574, the end of the volume): why those who follow the Torah are described as believers in Muhammad, the losers, the reminder of favours to the Children of Israel, and the warning of the day when no soul avails another, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu. Volume 2 of the Shakir edition is now fully harvested.
- Passage 2:121 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 2, pp. 566-571): the narrators' reports on reciting the Book 'as it ought to be recited' (following, acting by it, not altering it), his choice of 'follow it as it ought to be followed', the Kufan and Basran grammarians on adding 'haqq' to a definite noun, and the sense of 'those believe in it', each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:119 closes its al-Tabari commentary, and 2:120 and 2:121 gain their first (volume 2, pp. 561-566): his reading of 'will never be pleased with you', 'God's guidance is the guidance' and 'if you followed their desires', and the interpreters' views on who 'those to whom We gave the Book' are and what reciting it 'as it ought to be recited' means, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:118 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:119 gains its first (volume 2, pp. 557-561): 'We have made the signs clear', the sending with the truth, the two readings of 'you will not be asked about the companions of Hell' with the mursal reports and al-Tabari's reasons for the reading as a report, and the editor's notes on the chains and on Ibn Kathir's remark, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:118 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 2, pp. 551-556): his reasons for taking the Christians as those who 'do not know' and the Jews as 'those before them', the narrators' other views, the sense of 'lawla' and 'tashabahat', and his reading of the whole verse, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:117 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:118 gains its first (volume 2, pp. 546-551): his preferred reading of 'Be, and it is' as general, his arguments against the figurative reading, the grammar of 'fa-yakun', and the narrators' views on who said 'why does God not speak to us' (Christians, Jews, Arab polytheists), each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:117 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 2, pp. 541-546): the meaning of 'qada' with its Arabic usage, his question on when 'Be' is said and the interpreters' four views (particular, general by knowledge, particular in meaning, and a figure of speech), each attributed with the editor's notes, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:115 closes its al-Tabari commentary, and 2:116 and 2:117 gain their first (volume 2, pp. 536-541): 'all-encompassing, all-knowing', his reading of the claim of a son and the senses of 'qanitun' (obedient, acknowledging, standing) with each narrator's view, and the meaning of 'badi'' with his Arabic parallels, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:115 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 2, pp. 531-536): the report on the Negus with the editor's weakness note, his reading of the verse as neither abrogating nor abrogated and the senses it allows (travel and fear, the qibla anywhere, supplication), the interpreters' views on 'the face of God', and the verse's link to the preceding one, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:115 gains more of al-Tabari's commentary (volume 2, pp. 526-531): the interpreters' views on the occasion of 'to God belong the East and the West' (the change of prayer direction, the permission to face any way before the obligation, voluntary prayer on a mount, and people unsure of the direction), with the editor's notes on the chains and on Ibn Kathir's remark on 'no place is void of Him', each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:114 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:115 gains its first (volume 2, pp. 521-526): his reasons for taking the Christians as those meant in 2:114 with the editor's note on Ibn Kathir's objection, the reports on 'except fearing' and 'disgrace in this world', and the start of his reading of 'to God belong the East and the West', each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:113 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:114 gains its first (volume 2, pp. 516-521): the views on 'those who do not know', the judgement on the Day of Resurrection, the grammar of 'prevents the mosques of God', and the interpreters' differing views on who is meant (the Christians, Nebuchadnezzar and his helpers, or Quraysh at al-Hudaybiya) with al-Tabari's preference, each attributed to its narrator, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:112 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:113 gains its first (volume 2, pp. 511-516): the end of his reading of 'submits his face' and the singular and plural in the verse, the report of the Najran dispute, his reading of what each party said of the other and how it squares with their denial of the Prophet, and the reports of Qatada, Mujahid and Ibn Abbas, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passages 2:111 and 2:112 gain their first al-Tabari commentary (volume 2, pp. 507-511): how the Jews' and Christians' claims are joined in the report, the senses of 'hud', 'bring your proof' read as a denial of their claim, and the meaning of submitting one's face to God with his Arabic parallels, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:109 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:110 gains its first (volume 2, pp. 502-506): his reading of 'pardon and overlook' and the narrators' reports that it was later abrogated, the meaning of 'qadir', 'whatever good you send ahead' and why the believers were told to pray and give zakat, and 'God sees what you do' as promise and warning, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:108 ends and 2:109 gains its first al-Tabari commentary (volume 2, pp. 497-502): the straight path likeness, his reading of the address as to the Companions, the reports on who 'many of the People of the Book' are and his rejection of the single-person reading, the grammar of 'envy from themselves', and the reports on 'after the truth had become clear to them', each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:108 closes its al-Tabari commentary (volume 2, pp. 492-497): his view on the particle 'am', the sense of exchanging faith for disbelief and the Abu al-Aliya gloss, his reading of these verses as addressed to the Companions, and the sense of 'strayed' and 'the right path' with its road likeness, each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Passage 2:107 closes its al-Tabari commentary and 2:108 gains its first (volume 2, pp. 487-492): sovereignty versus ownership, the denial of the Jews who rejected abrogation, the reports on the occasion of the verse (Rafi' ibn Huraymila and Wahb ibn Zayd, the Arabs, Quraysh and al-Safa, Abu al-Aliya's mursal report with the editor's note), and the grammarians' views on 'am', each attributed, in Arabic, English and Urdu.

- **Persons**: 25 more records with full Arabic, English and Urdu prose moved from draft to published (prose-fill persons batch 53 — Ibn Hisham Sirah cohorts).
- Hidaya: added al-Tabari's commentary on "hudan lil-muttaqin" (pp. 230-233): guidance read as light for the God-fearing, the reports from Hasan and Ibn Abbas on taqwa, and the grammatical options for hudan, in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Concept al-Muqattaat (the disjoined letters at the openings of surahs) gains al-Tabari's survey of the views on "alif-lam-mim": names of the Qur'an, names of surahs, an oath, the words the letters stand for, and his conclusion that each letter carries many meanings; the abjad reckoning reported from Jewish rabbis is set aside as weakly sourced (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 205–220). Added in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- The Fatiha closes in al-Tabari's commentary across Arabic, English and Urdu: the reports identifying the sirat and the grammar of "ghayr al-maghdub", God's anger (ghadab), "nor of those who are astray", the reason the Fatiha is long and the hadith of the divided prayer, with Adi ibn Hatim's reports (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 171–201).
- **Persons**: 25 more records with full Arabic, English and Urdu prose moved from draft to published (prose-fill persons batch 52 — Ibn Hisham Sirah cohorts).
- Concepts Iyyaka na'budu wa-iyyaka nasta'in, Hidaya and Malik yawm al-din, and the person Ismail ibn Abd al-Rahman al-Suddi, gain al-Tabari's commentary on the Fatiha verses 4 to 6 in Arabic, English and Urdu: abasement and help in worship, the order and repetition of "iyyaka", guidance as steadfast help, the day of religion as the day of reckoning, and the chain that runs through al-Suddi (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 156–169).
- Concept al-Hamd lillah gains al-Tabari's treatment of praise and thanks (hamd and shukr, the article on hamd, and the reading of the nominative); a new concept al-Rabb sets out the three senses of rabb that al-Tabari joins in "Lord of the worlds"; Malik yawm al-din gains his preference for "king" over "owner" and his sense of din as reckoning (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 137–143, 149–155). Added in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- Concepts al-Hamd lillah, al-Rahman al-Rahim and Asma Allah al-Husna, and the persons Ata al-Khurasani and Abdullah ibn Abbas, gain al-Tabari's commentary on the opening of the Fatiha in Arabic, English and Urdu: the sense of praise and of "the worlds", the order of the divine names and the forbidden names, the second mention of al-Rahman al-Rahim in the Fatiha, and the Ata and Ibn Abbas reports (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 130–135, 143, 146–147).
- Concepts al-Rahman al-Rahim and Rahma gain al-Tabari's commentary on the two names in Arabic, English and Urdu: the grammar of rahman and rahim, why both are named, the general mercy of al-Rahman against the particular mercy of al-Rahim, and his rejection of the claim that the Arabs did not know the name (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 126–132). Al-Rahman al-Rahim moves to published.
- Concept al-Basmala gains al-Tabari's commentary in Arabic, English and Urdu: the verse-count dispute, the meaning of the phrase and the implied verb (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 109, 114–116). Status moved to published.
- Concept al-Sirat al-Mustaqim moved from draft to published with full Arabic, English and Urdu prose: al-Tabari's consensus that it is the clear road, his preferred reading of "guide us", and the link to the next verse (Shakir edition, volume 1, pp. 170–171).

- **25 new contemporary biographies**: preachers, teachers and Qur'an reciters followed by millions — from Pakistan, Indonesia, Bangladesh, Turkey, Egypt and France — each with full Arabic, English and Urdu pages and sources.

### Added
- **Another forty Timeline Explorer stories in preparation**: five places and thirty-five people selected from existing cited records, with explicit event curation and Arabic/English/Urdu subject metadata. Reserved shared-image defects are deferred to their owners rather than hidden by dropping events.
- **Forty Explorer stories implemented**: the final twenty person journeys complete the selection, with 185 chronological stops across 132 existing events. All 172 subject/event records pass citation checks; existing Urdu wording is preserved and missing subject translations supplied. No event facts, dates or relations changed.
- **Forty Explorer stories verified**: the full static build and all 120 Arabic/English/Urdu routes pass, including mobile kids imagery and details interactions. All 85 existing served image variants were screened; optional colour backgrounds and existing event-language fallbacks remain explicit. Concurrent harvest and biography work is preserved.
- **Explorer hand-off**: preserve the forty-story work and concurrent source enrichments in #3452, with validation/build snapshots and the final citation-only differences documented. Work stops after the founder-requested wind-down merge; no deployment or further batch is started.
- **Explorer completion resumed**: the founder reauthorized final verification and merging of the existing forty-story batch. Current biography publications are preserved, and final citation/output checks are rerun before shipping; no additional batch is started.
- **Explorer build blocker recorded**: #3476 documents a required tour stop displaced by the Time Browser's fixed-size sample after upstream content additions. All tests and validation pass, but the full build is blocked; reserved owner code remains untouched and #3452 is not claimed complete.
- **Required Time Browser tour stops stay available**: the founder-authorized fix for #3476 retains required, eligible tour stops within the existing 50-record sample even as content grows. Chronological order, unique records, the existing eligibility checks and ordinary samples that already contain the stops are preserved; regression tests cover the previously failing Kirman stop.
- **Next forty Explorer stories shipped**: #3416 merges the forty person journeys after integrated validation, citation checks and all 318 tests pass; sixteen screened backdrops are already merged in #3414. Completion claims and lane records now name the actual merges. No production or UAT deployment was run.
- **Next forty Explorer journeys verified**: 270 chronological stops across existing cited events, with 120 Arabic/English/Urdu routes checked in the production build and browser. Sixteen new screened backdrops, merged separately in #3414, remove this batch's legacy illustration fallbacks; existing event-language gaps and reserved owner follow-ups remain explicit.
- **Next Timeline Explorer batch, final twenty person stories**: complete the forty-subject definitions and translated subject metadata. Owner-blocked Hamza and Usama proposals are deferred without changing their records or shared images; Abu Sa'id al-Khudri supplies the final slot.
- **Next Timeline Explorer batch, first twenty person stories**: prepare cited chronological journeys with Arabic, English and Urdu subject metadata, reusing existing events rather than inventing dates or links. The preceding forty-story batch is merged in #3400; the new batch retains explicit source-data and image-owner follow-ups.
- **Sixteen native Timeline Explorer backdrops**: eight new adult/kids pairs replace legacy record-illustration fallbacks with screened empty-place scenes. Existing shared images and record illustrations are preserved; these assets ship separately from the next forty stories.
- **Timeline Explorer verification**: the forty new stories pass the full local build and all 120 language-route browser checks; the authorized image correction is merged separately in #3398, resolving the earlier publication blocker. Existing event-language fallbacks and map-selector follow-ups are documented rather than claimed fixed.
- **Safer Timeline Explorer scenery**: ten new adult/kids backdrops override five legacy illustrated scenes, and five shared backdrops with figures are replaced by empty landscapes after explicit founder approval. All fifteen images were visually screened at full size; historical facts and site code are unchanged.
- **Developers page** (`/{lang}/developers/`, Arabic, English, Urdu): the museum's open data and API documented for libraries and builders — endpoints, the node/edge/record schema, worked examples with real responses, sample code, starter prompts for AI coding agents, and an honest list of what is not here yet.
- **Network page** (`/{lang}/network/`): the scholars, institutions and communities in conversation with the museum.
- **Canary release notes**: this file, the changelog page that renders it, and the site version now shown in the footer of every page — so any reader can watch the museum change and know exactly which version they are looking at.
- **A standing rule for every agent** (in the repo's `AGENTS.md`): anyone who changes anything in this repository records the change here in the same commit, then commits, opens a pull request and merges it — finished work is never left sitting locally.
- **Forty more Timeline Explorer stories**: follow twenty places and twenty people through 303 dated, cited stops drawn from existing museum records. Every new subject has Arabic, English and Urdu titles and summaries; individual event text retains the documented Arabic fallback where a translation is missing.

### Fixed
- **Shared Explorer image correction authorized**: five previously blocked backdrops now have screened, people-free replacements following explicit founder approval. The image batch ships separately from the forty new stories; historical dates, facts, relationships and UI code are unchanged.
- **Five Explorer fallback scenes**: ten new adult/kids landscape backdrops prevent five legacy record illustrations with figures from being selected at those stops. Every new image was inspected at full size; the legacy files and other owners' Explorer assets were left unchanged.

### Tests
- **Forty-story Explorer checkpoint**: all 120 built Arabic, English and Urdu routes have been checked in the browser, including desktop/mobile controls and the new Fadak kids backdrop. Local validation, citation checks, 318 tests and the complete site build pass; publication remains blocked by the five shared-image defects tracked in issue #3350.

### Data
- **Clearer subject metadata**: Urdu labels and summaries added to forty existing records, with recorded localization provenance. Missing provenance restored on seven places and one migration event without changing existing event dates or relationships.
- **Honest Explorer image status**: the forty-story batch remains unpublished pending replacement of five shared backdrops that contain figures. The owner request identifies each file and affected story; a prompt or an earlier screening claim is not treated as proof that an image is safe.

### Data
- **Two scholars' pages completed**: Fayz Kashani, the Safavid philosopher and traditionist, and Şeyhülislam Zekeriya Efendi now carry full sourced biographies in Arabic, English and Urdu — both were early draft cards that other pages already linked to.

### Data
- **Nine more contemporary voices, and two broadcasters**: new sourced pages for Saliha Erdim, Ning Umi Laila, Firanda Andirja, Hayati İnanç, Nihat Hatipoğlu, Gus Baha, Osman Nuri Topbaş, Gus Mus and Gus Muwafiq — preachers, teachers and writers that Indonesian and Turkish audiences learn from today — plus the Indonesian dawah networks Yufid.TV and Rodja TV, each in Arabic, English and Urdu.

### Data
- **Twenty-one more contemporary voices**: new sourced pages for Malaysia's Ebit Lew, Wadi Annuar, Asma Harun, Norhafizah Musa and Firdaus Wong Wai Hung; South Korea's Daud Kim; Kazakhstan's Kabylbek Alipbek, Nurlan Baizhigitov, Nursultan Rysmaganbet, Arman Kuanyshbayev and Yersin Amre and Uzbekistan's Abror Muxtor Aliy; Albania's Elvis Naçi and Ahmed Kalaja, Bosnia's Elvedin Pezić, Kosovo's Shefqet Krasniqi and Enis Rama, and Türkiye's Halil Konakçı; Somalia's Mustafe Haji Ismail, Tanzania's Nurdeen Kishki and Mali's Ousmane Madani Haïdara — preachers and teachers their audiences learn from today, each in Arabic, English and Urdu.
- **A record brought up to date**: Adnan Rashid's page now names the Sapience Institute as his current affiliation, with iERA recorded as his earlier post.

### Fixed
- **Release notes recovered**: this changelog file was emptied by a merge accident on a parallel branch; its full text through 4 November is restored here from the last intact copy in the repository's own history, so the public changelog is complete again.

### Data
- **Six dawah media organisations**: new sourced pages for Kazakhstan's ÁLI stýdıasy studio channel, the TALIM KZ channel and the Hazret Sultan Mosque's official channel; Kenya-based Somali-language producer Hadaf Production and the Somali community channel Safiirka Islaamka; and Tanzania's Khidmatulquran Islamic Foundation, organiser of national Qur'an competitions since 2004 — each in Arabic, English and Urdu.

## v1.0.2 — 2026-10-31

### Added
- **Disclaimer page** in all three editions, linked from the footer: what the museum is, how its content is gathered and checked, and the terms on which it is offered.
- **Raisely** added as a fifth voluntary support channel on the Support page.
- **Dataset archived on Zenodo** (27 Oct): the museum's CC0 dataset with a permanent DOI — 10.5281/zenodo.23692511 — so libraries and researchers can cite and keep it forever.

---

## v1.0.1 — 2026-10-17

### Added
- **Kids' wing** («جناح الصغار»): a Hudhud-guided children's wing — Word Garden, Prophet Quest, Kindness Quest, bedtime stories and a teacher's kit — in Arabic and English.

---

## v1.0.0 — 2026-10-11

### Added
- **Public launch of islam.museum**: an Arabic-first digital museum of Islamic history with an English edition, nine wings, and a sourced knowledge graph of people, places, buildings, objects, works, passages, concepts and events — published the same day the domain was registered.
